you're short on time, so i'll try to help you. we are talking about genetically modified organisms today, but we are talking about something much bigger. we are talking about more, about the self-determination of sovereign states. as former u.s. president ronald reagan said, if a country does not control its borders, then you cannot speak of a country. the member states in the eu are not countries. but it goes further, because they also have no control over the spending of their tax money. they have no legislation of their own. so whether it is the admission of budgetary rules, the admission of foreigners, or the admission of gmos, the eu should not interfere. that is up to the member states themselves. for years we in europe have stressed the importance of knowledge and innovation. innovations in the field of genetic modification hold great promise and can lead to a better and more efficient food supply. i am therefore concerned that the european cultivation of new gm crops has become a sham. our agriculture is lagging behind. biotech companies, which provide many highly skilled jobs, look elsewhere for salvation. i hope that this new legislation will provide more scope and allow for a better start to the licensing process. if this does not succeed, new measures will have to be taken. the choice is up to the member states: let us think in terms of opportunities and not in terms of risks. european agriculture is still one of the most innovative in the world. let's keep it that way.
in writing. the ecr group abstained on my proposal in the vote on the fisheries partnership agreement between the eu and são tomé and príncipe. before voting on that agreement, we felt that it was going too far. for although this relatively small agreement contains positive elements, including more extensive support for local fisheries, unfortunately it lacks an important aspect. the agreement does not contain any agreements on the protection of sharks, whereas bycatch of sharks is a major problem in these waters. i am disappointed that this is not sufficiently acknowledged by the european commission. it is a missed opportunity that the agreement does not include any conservation measures to reduce by-catch of sharks. i hope that the european commission will keep an eye on this issue when implementing this agreement and ensure that the wider international agreements on shark bycatch in tuna fishing are respected. also in the waters off the island group of são tomé and príncipe.
in writing. while i welcome the return to national governments of a power that should never have been taken from them by the eu, i voted against this proposal because it does not provide sufficient legal guarantees for states to ban genetically modified organisms (gmos) on their own territory.
in writing. these new rules further open the european door to genetically modified products and leave member states with too few options to ban genetically modified crops (gmos). biotech companies will use a divide and rule tactic. if gmos are authorised in some member states, other countries will have to follow quickly to avoid disrupting the internal market. 90% of europeans do not want genetically modified crops in their fields, let alone in their food. member states should be given extensive and legally guaranteed options to ban these crops on their own, even if brussels does allow them. the party for the animals has for years opposed genetic manipulation, partly because it leads to more monocultures, whereas there is a lot of effort to increase the diversity of species. in addition, these gmos will increase the use of agricultural pesticides, further degrading the quality of soil, groundwater and surface water. we must instead focus on cleaner, organic farming, which does not rely on agricultural pesticides. that is why i voted against this bill.

the mediterranean is one of the most beautiful seas in the world, but it is threatened by overfishing, by-catch and the plundering of coral reefs. the commission proposal was largely focused on restoring ecosystems, but did not exclude fisheries. i have proposed that fishing be banned altogether in the most vulnerable areas. this offers a chance for overfished fish species to survive and prevents many other species from being by-catched. trawling in particular is responsible for a high incidence of by-catches of dolphins, seabirds and other mammals, which are caught in the nets and subsequently drown. scientific advice has been given to limit trawls. i asked for a total ban because this is much more controllable. i also proposed to prevent the plundering of coral reefs by banning the harvesting and sale of red coral, also called blood coral. coral reefs are threatened because much of the coral is used to make jewelry. the underwater world of coral reefs is unique and deserves our protection. unfortunately, many of the amendments were aimed at broadening the commission's proposal rather than tightening it up. i voted against this very weakened proposal.
the european summit on the investment package was supposed to last two days, but it lasted about two hours. you could say that the countries were like the chickens in the henhouse to miss their chance. the member states have a fine line on jobs and growth, but they forget to put it into practice, namely reforming their economies, but certainly also freeing up money for private and public investment. only with contributions from countries can the juncker investment programme have a sufficient impact. the economic policies of the member states now resemble those of the gold standard. this is not a good omen for the euro as a currency and certainly not for the european citizens. they are paying the price for low growth and high unemployment. i therefore ask the commission and the president of the european council, donald tusk, to urge the european countries to seize their opportunity.

for the ppe group the relationship and communication between a client and his lawyer is of a special nature and the confidentiality of that relationship and communication is crucial to maintaining citizens' trust in the rule of law and thus also crucial to the functioning of our democracy. at the same time, this is not to say that such information should never be collected, that it is impossible or undesirable. i believe that wiretapping of conversations between clients and lawyers should be possible in very exceptional cases, when it is covered by sufficient safeguards and when it would be in the specific demonstrable interest of national or public security. that right, in the context of national security, to collect information about even this very special kind of conversation is also crucial for member states to guarantee the safety of their citizens. i wonder what exactly the purpose of this debate is here tonight. as the council and the commission have already pointed out, this is a national competence. the supervisory board for the intelligence and security services in the netherlands has issued a comprehensive opinion. it has been made public and presented to the national parliament. please let the national security and intelligence services and also the national parliaments do their job.
the european parliament has called on the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the eu's citizens are able to vote in elections in their country of residence. that trust has been shamed by the revelations in the large-scale wiretapping scandals. it was only a matter of time before it became clear that somewhere in europe a secret service was tapping into a law firm for a long time. and it has become the netherlands, where the minister of the interior, ronald plasterk, admits that he has been listening to the law firm prakken d'oliveira in amsterdam for a long time. this is about the foundation of our rule of law, this is about our trust in government. if a conversation with a lawyer is no longer safe, what about a conversation with a doctor, or anyone else with a trade secret? the council says this is a national matter, but i say: no, it's not that. because there is a european directive from 2013 on the right to a lawyer, of which article 4 states that there is an obligation to guarantee confidentiality. there is also the charter of fundamental rights, which guarantees the right to a fair trial. this is not a simple national jurisdiction. i would therefore ask you, mrs jourová, and through you also mr timmermans, who is very familiar with the dutch minister of the interior: will you take the dutch authorities to court for not complying with the law and will you oblige the dutch authorities and the other member states to respect the confidentiality between client and lawyer?

i am fully in favour of the juncker plan, for investment and for taking steps towards a real economy. but i do have a few comments. first of all, i think it's too much of a stand-alone plan. we have 460 billion real money in our structural and investment funds and the relationship between those funds and the plan is completely unclear. i also think that much more needs to be done. we have experience with risk sharing facilities, jeremy programs, etc. so much more can be done with the existing financial provisions in our budgets. secondly, i am happy about the additionality. we must be very careful that the debt already decentralised in various projects is not taken over by the eib and does not generate additional real investment. the criteria are actually very vague. my question to the council and to you is this: now, suppose the money ends up in the old member states and not in the new ones. is that acceptable? 80 to 90% end up in some member states and not in others. is that what you're talking about? and may i also mention the crossborder projects? that is what europe is for. do these types of projects get priority? can the council give an answer to that?

president, ladies and gentlemen, in recent years the eu has been hit by several meat scandals. consumers were shocked, and rightly so. food safety must be guaranteed and such fraud must be tackled with all the necessary vigour. the european parliament's call for the labelling of processed meat products by origin will not prevent fraud, however. if someone wants to sell horse meat for beef, a label is not going to stop him. europe has some of the strictest food safety standards in the world. all products manufactured or imported into europe must comply with these standards. the meat processing industry uses different meat suppliers from several countries. there are also frequent changes of supplier, which would require a constant adjustment of a label. this makes the regulation practically unworkable. the commission's investigation shows that this measure increases production costs by up to 50%. such costs are borne not only by the trader but also by the consumer. i am therefore not in favour of mandatory labelling of the origin of meat in processed products. producers may voluntarily put this on their labels if they wish to do so and if they can distinguish themselves on the market. in conclusion, i would like to say that the call seems to be sympathetic, but it is the wrong instrument. this is unnecessary rule-making that does not solve the real problems. (the president agreed to answer a question in the form of a 'blue card' (rule 162 (8)).)
"blue card" is the answer. mr wojciechowski, thank you for your question. it may surprise you, but no consumer thinks the same. so many people, so many desires. one wants to know the origin, the other does not want to pay extra for it if the origin is indicated on the label. i am therefore in favour of not imposing origin labelling; producers may indicate the origin on a voluntary basis. and if you're such a supporter of labeling your meat, then you just buy products that say it.
mr president, every year millions of animals are dragged across europe like sledgehammers. these animals may be packed for days on end in crowded trains, cattle cars, planes, or boats. their journey usually ends at the slaughterhouse. why would you do that? because slaughtering in romania is a few cents cheaper. or because parma ham has to be made in parma from fresh ham. a proper origin label with information on the place of birth, rearing, upbringing and slaughter of the animals makes these absurd animal transports visible. such a label gives consumers the opportunity to choose genuine local products and makes meat and meat fraud more traceable. we are very much in favour of this proposal. we would also like to see information on animal welfare, which is totally lacking as far as we are concerned, and we would like to see the meat of more species, including horse meat, added. i also believe that agricultural subsidies should be abolished! (the speaker agrees to answer a question in the form of a 'blue card' (rule 162 (8)). )
"blue card" is the answer. thank you for asking. it is clear that consumers want that diversification too. they prefer products that are made in their own region, so they know where they came from. at the moment it is absolutely impossible to see where the salami or the pizza was produced and under what conditions. we think it is important that you put that on the label too, so that consumers simply have the opportunity to make a really good choice without having to go through long animal transports beforehand.

madam president, in june 2012 the international organisation of employers walked into the ilo conference with their corporate lawyers in a grossly unprecedented move. after decades of jurisprudence, they challenged the fact that the right to strike was part of the ilo convention on the freedom of association, and social dialogue on the international level has been gridlocked ever since. these employers want to take away the only tool workers have in order to stand up for themselves. after the catastrophe in dhaka, bangladesh, in 2013 thousands of workers swamped the streets to demand safer working conditions. because of the right to strike they were able to stand up for safe working conditions and against fraudulent employers. also in europe, we have seen workers’ rights being weakened by the crisis: social dialogue is under pressure and unions are increasingly being set aside. this attack on the right to strike is a direct attack on fundamental social rights in the eu and abroad. without the right to strike, collective bargaining is nothing more than collective begging. dear commissioner, i heard your support for the routes for bringing this case to the international court of justice, and i applaud your intentions. my question is what can you do in order to get more votes for these routes?

in writing. i voted against the sustainable fisheries partnership agreement between the european union and senegal for three obvious reasons: - all fisheries agreements between the eu and developing countries are designed to maximise the exploitation of seas and oceans in order to cover up the failure of the european fisheries policy and to keep the european fishing fleet afloat. this is at the expense of animals, nature, the environment and the people on the ground. destructive european fisheries policy cannot be sustained by fishing more or elsewhere; it is better to clean up the mess and stop the subsidised overfishing. - west african waters are already severely overfished, while illegal fishing and lack of research on fish stocks make up-to-date data unavailable. this is particularly true of the vulnerable bluefin tuna. it is irresponsible to let european vessels fish there; - senegal itself is in the process of introducing new legislation to reduce their fishing pressure; it is a contradiction to agree on a fisheries partnership agreement at this particular moment.
in writing. - the vvd commends the us senate for the very objective and candid report on the cia's role in waterboarding, rendition, etc. it is welcome if the eu member states and other countries would mirror the us approach. the vvd has therefore supported the cia resolution, which means that it is the judiciary in the member states which ultimately decides whether people are prosecuted and governments and parliaments themselves who decide what lessons they draw from this affair.
in writing. — the greens/efa group today voted against the joint motion for a resolution on anti-terrorism measures, as the resolution is sending the wrong message at the wrong time. the resolution agreed by the 4 bigger political groups provides carte blanche for eu governments to scale back personal freedoms with measures that will fail to properly respond to the terrorist threat. instead of pushing for effective measures, the usual suspects who have been continually pushing for mass surveillance and data retention are using the paris attacks as an excuse to bulldoze through these measures, despite any evidence that they will prove effective in countering the terrorist threat. a coherent counter-terrorism strategy would focus on targeted surveillance of real suspects and ensuring security authorities have the means and resources to focus on specific risks and concrete suspicions, to follow any leads immediately, and to efficiently exchange information about dangerous individuals. this is what we should be trying to achieve. there is also a need to tackle radicalisation in schools and prisons. finally we are missing a call for a legal definition of profiling. stepping up mass surveillance will undermine security and is instead a victory for fundamentalists, who are combatting precisely such democratic freedoms.
in writing. - the vvd is convinced of the need to cooperate very closely within the eu and also with other countries, such as the usa, in combating and preventing terrorism. the vvd does not believe that it is necessary to create new institutions or to allocate new community powers at the present time, since each country is best placed to combat and prevent terrorism on its own territory, especially in the cities. the need for the close cooperation referred to above has led the vvd, despite its reservations about a central role for brussels, to vote in favour of the anti-terrorism resolution.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i have to say that i am a bit disappointed because commissioner timmermans said that we do not need a protracted institutional debate about mechanisms. i think that is exactly what we need because the fundamental rights and the fundamental values that this union is based on are laid down in article 2 of the treaties. it is the most important article. it is the highest article in the hierarchy and yet the enforcement of that article is the weakest of all the articles in the treaty. i remember the debates about the eurozone. we said at the time that we did not need binding instruments in the eurozone, we have a gentleman’s agreement and all the member states will simply have to live up to their commitments. well, we have seen what that led to. i think it is about time that this european union, which is developing into a political union, equips itself with the instruments to enforce those values that it is so proud of. we should not only be talking – not only words, no matter if they are in five or six languages – but we should have action in all the languages. we do need binding instruments, not just conventions, not just silent diplomacy, colloquia – we need binding instruments. that is why my group, the alde group, has proposed the democratic governance pact. we have offered it to the commissioner and we have debated it since. it can be adopted on the basis of existing treaties and would be similar to the stability and growth pact. it would have similar mechanisms to ensure the enforcement of the values that are all dear to us. for example, we have devised one instrument – and i will show it to you, it looks like a bingo card, it is actually a scoreboard. we have scored all 28 member states against a set of indicators, like freedom of speech, freedom of assembly, equal treatment, freedom of religion, etc. we have been nice and we have not put the names of the member states here. i can only tell you that the ones with only green scores are denmark and sweden but the rest we will keep anonymous. if we did this exercise with parliament, with the council, the commission and with the national parliaments every year – and here i take up your proposal for an annual debate simultaneously in all the institutions about the state of democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights in all member states, because there i concur very much with the commissioner – all member states should be treated equally and should be scrutinised in the same way. there would be no more debates about single member states here, or indeed monitoring mechanisms for bulgaria and romania as we have them, or only the candidate member states. no, all member states should be scrutinised. we will scrutinise not just their budget. we will not talk only about debt and budget deficits but will also talk about the enforcement of our shared values and make the european union a community of values.

in writing. in a world on fire, where conflicts cross borders with no clear separation between good and evil forces, we must look for solutions. so far, military intervention in distant countries with cultures different from our own has mainly exacerbated conflicts and left countries in chaos. who are the coalition partners? who exactly are our allies and enemies and which countries are in double digits? what is the best way to combat acts of terrorism? is that by jet fighters or are there other means? we will have to monitor the situation in syria closely with the international legal community, but if we really want to show compassion for the victims of the civil war, we must first of all help syrian refugees. the sense of anger and powerlessness that we all feel is understandable but does not justify rash military action. fear is a bad counselor, but feelings of anger and retribution are just as bad.
if we have learned anything in the last few months, it is that the european union is first and foremost a political project. and a political project of vital importance, now that it's clear that geopolitics is back from the dead. a crucial tool in geopolitics is energy. we have seen this again this week with the bizarre gas deal that hungarian prime minister orban is seeking to conclude with russia, and the threat by gazprom to stop gas supplies via ukraine. but apart from europe's enormous dependence on energy, we have two other problems: our energy is too polluting and too expensive. only a strong european energy union can solve these three energy problems. unity not only gives strength in relations with third countries, but also gives clarity and certainty to investors. billions are waiting to be invested in europe's energy infrastructure. we must create the right conditions for this, in order to create the necessary smart and large networks. my group very much welcomes the emphasis which the commission is placing on the further completion of the internal energy market. but then we'll have to act. unlike the previous commission, the current team must have the courage to bite when member states fail to implement legislation. my group is critical of the governance proposals. of course, this is sensitive in some member states, but let us not make the same mistake as in economic policy, where the worst financial crisis since the 1930s was needed to give europe the right resources. let us learn from that lesson and choose the community at once. finally, over the next few years, more than one trillion, or a thousand billion euros, will be invested in energy infrastructure. those investments have to be good right away, so they're targeted at that low-carbon economy of tomorrow. i think the european commission should play a more active role in this.

president. - we are now closing a long dossier on weights and measures. it is good that we have now reached an agreement and that modern designed trucks now have a chance for the future: the commission has already proposed a number of amendments to the directive. that's a step in the right direction. yet i stand here with mixed feelings. we could have done much more to make the road transport sector more environmentally friendly and safer, while reducing congestion on european roads. i am referring, of course, to the ecocombi, those longer and heavier trucks that we have known for a long time from the dutch roads and on the dutch roads. we see the ecocombs there driving around to everyone's satisfaction. the environmental performance is better, there are fewer trucks on the road, there is no negative impact on the maintenance costs of the infrastructure, nor is there any negative impact on road safety. all the studies on the ecocombi show that. so you would expect europe to take up this opportunity and give the ecocombi a wide berth. such an innovative concept, who'd leave it alone? but alas, what do we see? unfounded prejudices and fear of innovation are at the forefront of this parliament and so the eco-combi does not stand a chance in europe. we therefore remain dependent on the goodwill of a member state to allow an ecocomb. fortunately, there is a back door, because we have known since 2012 that the european commission is fine with the ecocomb being used across borders, provided that both countries on the border side allow the ecocomb at the national level. i'm glad there's a back door, but we missed a chance today. the ecocombi should have come to europe. blue card reply. this is a very astute question because we, as the european parliament, are all working towards better intermodality. we want transport to move away from air and road, towards rail and inland waterways. but at some point you see that it's easier to move, say, vegetables, fresh fruit, food, door to door quickly in large quantities, while, say, sand, steel, concrete, can keep going through the ship. intermodality is therefore also co-modality, i.e. the different modes side by side, and the ecocombi fits into that.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs and security, on the situation in ukraine. i am very concerned about the role of the eu in this. president juncker is calling for a european army with a warlike spirit. he sounds like that other chairman, chairman mao, who with his red army annexed the vulnerable tibet. under juncker, it is becoming increasingly clear that the eu has an aggressive enlargement agenda. but the commission is making a serious error of assessment: russia is not tibet. president putin is not the dalai lama. juncker supports poroshenko, a war criminal who has bombed civilians in lugansk, and on the same day that russian opposition leader nemtsov was assassinated, poroshenko's main opponent, mr chechetov, was found dead. the european council should call for a war tribunal for the crimes against humanity in ukraine, for which poroshenko is also responsible.
the situation in the european union is a disaster. there is no or little economic growth. the debts have never been so high and they are growing by the day. unemployment is immense, and there is no improvement in sight. the european central bank has made interest rates extremely low and has started the money press, and as a result the countries that do not want to reform will never do so again. there's no way out of this situation unless we change the rules of the game. the euro must be dismantled. the member states must decide their own economic policies. but this parliament now wants to make an unaffordable welfare state compulsory in all member states. at whose expense? what we do need is small governments, small debts, low taxes. that alone is the recipe for restoring economic growth. we will not need the economic semester that is on the agenda now.
mr president, the european economy seems to be getting better. the growth is accelerating. to me, that's a signal that the course is the right one. a budget in order, structural reforms and investment, but in that order and especially not 'either/or', because i hear that too often here 'either/or'. no, that is what europe needs. of course, we also need to invest in order to strengthen our economy, but we must do so in such a way that we do not allow the deficits and the public debt to increase again at a rapid pace. that is a mistake we have made too often in the past, we have underused the good years to build up buffers that we could use in the lean years that followed. we owe it to ourselves not to repeat that mistake and, more importantly, we owe it to our children not to saddle them with unsustainable debt. finally, this debate is also about the confidence of the european citizens. that trust is under pressure as they see france getting an exception to the rule again and again. let us treat all countries equally, whether they are large or small.
in writing. ‒ the rapporteur believes that the eu budget for 2016 should focus on improving the economy. this is a very good thing, even though the biggest obstacle is the euro and it is the member states that have to take the most measures to achieve a lasting economic recovery. the budgetary guidelines, however, have such problematic elements that they do not receive my support. the guidelines too easily treat expenditure as investment, and call for an increase in expenditure and especially payments. a wrong answer to the payment delay. they also envisage special instruments outside the multiannual financial framework and more european own resources. the opposite is necessary: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in addition, europe must no longer be given its own resources. the contributions of the member states reflect the fact that the union is at the service of the member states. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council. the guidelines are also in line with the critical assessment of the agencies on need, quality and scope for savings. i call on the european parliament to show real responsibility on these issues. the european parliament has already said that it will not be able to vote on the report.

member of the commission.– mr president, honourable members, on behalf of my colleague commissioner vella, who unfortunately cannot be here with you today, i would like to thank mr cadec as chair of the committee of fisheries for his initiative to have a resolution on sea bass, and all members who will support it. the sea bass stock in the southern north sea, the english channel, the celtic sea and the irish sea has been declining rapidly since 2012 and will, according to scientific advice, approach its historically lowest level in 2016. the concrete proposals in your motion for a resolution to establish a coordinated approach to the management of sea bass are therefore very important. for the past three years the commission has worked very hard to broker an agreement between member states on improved management of this stock. in 2012 the commission proposed the introduction of total allowable catch (tac) and has had ongoing discussions with member states. unfortunately member states were unable to agree, mainly because they could not agree on how to share the tac between them. last november the commission proposed alternative management measures, notably limiting the number of days at sea, freezing the number of licences for commercial fishermen and limiting the daily catches for recreational fishermen, all to contain fishing pressure on the stock. again the council was not able to come to an agreement on the commission’s proposal. considering the state of the resources and the urgency of the situation, the commission has subsequently decided to take temporary emergency measures for pelagic vessels targeting spawning aggregations of sea bass as part of a response to the state of the stock. these emergency measures are the first element in a package of envisaged measures which the commission plans to be adopted for the management of sea bass in 2015. the second element in this package is a commission proposal to limit the catches of recreational anglers to three fish per angler per day. recreational fishermen produce a quarter of all landings of sea bass and it is therefore important and justified that they contribute to decreasing the pressure on this stock. this proposal is now with the council, which is looking very favourably at the proposal. the adoption of these rules by the council is scheduled for 16 march. the commission is currently discussing the third element of the package with the member states concerned. this would cover several parts of the commercial sector which also impact on sea bass. it may take the form of catch limits. once this initial package is in place, and in order to rebuild the stock, we are envisaging including sea bass in the multiannual plan for the western atlantic quotas, as foreseen under the new common fisheries policy (cfp). we fully agree with you that in the preparation of such plans we need to consult all stakeholders, build on their knowledge and ensure a fair approach. we will also need to integrate the latest scientific advice on sea bass in the preparation of that plan.
i am also very concerned about the position of the sea bass and rightly so, the european commission has recently intervened and taken a number of measures to protect the sea bass. i am a little less happy with the resolution now on the table and especially with the way it has been put forward. i would have preferred that we had a good debate in the committee on fisheries and had weighed up all the pros and cons. the shortcut we're taking now is actually a little short of the curve. this is also reflected in the content of the resolution, because when i read in it that there should be a quota for sea bass, i think that this is not a good management mechanism for sea bass. for many small-scale fishermen, sea bass is a real hit of chance and if we come up with a quota, it will bring a lot of political struggle and bureaucratic hassle, whereas we want to reduce the bureaucratic pressure for fisheries. the most important thing is that we take sustainable measures to protect the sea bass during spawning and the spawning areas.
mr president, almost 90% of all fish species are severely overfished, and that includes sea bass. the situation is very bad for this species and that is why brussels has also taken emergency measures, but these measures could not have had enough effect. besides, they're due on april 30th. it is important to extend those emergency measures, at least until a multiannual management plan with additional conservation measures for the sea bass is in place. this species can live up to 30 years. it's a slow-growing species, it reproduces less quickly than a lot of other species, and so it also needs more time. i therefore call on my colleagues to vote in favour of the resolution to protect the sea bass, to vote in favour of the amendment calling for additional measures and for the extension of the emergency measures. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
president. - the united nations human rights council is at the heart of the global response to human rights violations. i am therefore very proud that my own country, the netherlands, has been elected to the council for the next three years. but we must not underestimate the task we have to do both in the human rights council and in the eu. the human rights situation worldwide has unfortunately reached a low point. every day, there are serious violations of human rights around the world. opposition leaders are being jailed without due process, human rights defenders and journalists must fear for their lives just for daring to be critical. discrimination and violence against lgbt people are commonplace, and tens of thousands of refugees still drown in the mediterranean every year. it's up to us to turn the tide. we can only do this by consistently addressing human rights issues in our external action, while remaining critical of ourselves. i would like to draw commissioner hahn's attention to the situation in azerbaijan, where many prominent human rights defenders and members of the opposition have been imprisoned in the past year. the case of leyla yunus, nominated by this parliament for the sakharov prize, is a symbol of the repressive nature of the government in baku. the first step has been taken with the priorities we have formulated for the 28th un human rights council. i therefore call on the commission, parliament and the member states to pursue this course together.

the roma community is the largest ethnic minority in europe, with 10 million people. their daily lives are systematically made difficult by discrimination, xenophobia and stigmatisation. it is clear that this is not just a problem of eastern european countries, but of the european union as a whole. a sad but telling example occurred three months ago when the mayor of a suburb of paris refused to allow a dead roma baby to be buried in his commune. in recent years, a number of political measures and initiatives have been taken, including at european level. focused on housing, education and care, these measures aimed to improve the lives of roma in europe. unfortunately, that did not happen. the situation of roma today is still deplorably bad in all eu member states. this will continue as long as discrimination and exclusion are not addressed. there is no need for new working groups, strategies or large conferences. it is high time that we actually got to work. the mentality and prejudices towards roma must be addressed. in addition, member states should be consulted on their policies towards minorities and respect for fundamental rights. the segregation of roma is unacceptable. the european community of values is based on fundamental rights, human dignity, equality and the rights of minorities. it is high time that these words were made real to the 10 million roma in europe.

the baltic sea is a unique area. at the same time, we see here the sad example of the european fisheries policy. everything that's wrong with that is in the baltic sea. the trend has been the same for years: less fish every year! the biodiversity is still declining. almost all fish stocks are overfished. the cod, for example, is even seriously threatened with extinction. time and again, member states set catch limits that are far above the scientific advice. we hope that this proposal will put an end to this at least. as if overfishing were not enough, we also see that other environmental effects threaten the biodiversity of the baltic sea, such as animal manure, which allows a huge number of algae to grow. that is why i believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.

on behalf of the verts/ale group.– mr president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, nils torvalds, for his work on this file which indeed, as has been stated before, was not an easy file at all. certainly it was not easy to build up a majority in this house and then undertake negotiations with the council, and i think we have to thank the rapporteur for all his work in that. having said that, i am disappointed with the result but i am more disappointed that the council is not even present at this discussion. it somewhat shows the problem we are having here, that within the council the member states do not really see the problem at hand. that is, i think, a very bad signal they are giving here: that again they are just not showing interest. that was also very clear during the negotiations and i think that is a problem. why are we discussing this? we set a target of 10% for biofuels in the transport sector, but we all know that, if you look at how it has been developing, it mainly concerns the development of agri-fuels, of first-generation biofuels, which are not delivering the climate benefits that they are supposed to do. that is why we had to do something about it. the commission came forward with a proposal trying to cap the first generation at 5%. in parliament we managed at least to get a deal for a cap at around 6%, but the member states wanted this to be at 7%. unfortunately, in the negotiations, we lost this. in the end, we as a parliament, agreed also with the 7% cap which is, from my perspective, way too high, because this is just filling up the target with the bad biofuels which are not the ones that we want to stimulate; we want to stimulate the more advanced ones. that was the second demand of parliament, that we want advanced biofuels in order to really have innovation in europe. unfortunately, here also the council did not want anything. in the end, we have a non-binding target for advanced fuels, so that means that there is a very weak signal for stimulating innovation within the european union. and if you look at the definitions, this term is also poorly defined and now we will probably have a debate where everyone will call his own product ‘advanced biofuel’. suddenly everyone will be part of that new hype but not really delivering innovation. the third problem relates to indirect land use change. we wanted clear accounting of indirect land use change and we lost that too; we only have reporting, not accounting. in that sense, from my perspective, we lost too much. we just lost too much as a parliament, and therefore i cannot support this deal. but the commissioner is here and i would like to ask what are we going to do after 2020? are we going to learn from this problem and not set a very inflexible new target but to put the fuel quality directive at centre stage: setting a target on reduction, and not specifically targeting a technology when we do not know exactly how it will develop? therefore the question: will we continue after 2020 with the fuel quality directive? if we continue with the directive, we will have a new debate and then i hope that the council will be present because this is a very important topic. if we do it right we can really have decarbonisation in the transport sector, but if we do it wrong we are stimulating bad technologies and that is what we would like to prevent. blue-card answer.– first of all, biofuels are not the only alternative in town. that is one of the biggest problems; we are now stimulating just one technology in the transport sector that can decarbonise the transport sector, whereas there are many other options. so i think that what we have done in our policies is focus too much on one technology, which is a very stupid thing to do from a political perspective. so you have to set your central objective, your decarbonisation, and then have different options for how to play this game. in that sense, what we have been doing now is really focusing on one bad technology that does not deliver; does that mean that we are fixating ourselves on fossil fuels? no, the problem is that now some of these alternatives are even worse for the climate than the fossil fuels, and then we are really stimulating the wrong one. that we really need to fix, and unfortunately we are not fixing that enough in this case. blue-card answer. – first of all, mr fjellner, i am voting in favour of the envi mandate. i know you were against. you did not want anything on indirect land use change. you want a higher cap for the first generation. you have been taken in by the agriculture lobby for a long while. we know that. if you really wanted to have some change then you would have joined the envi deal. you were not part of that deal. in the negotiations you were ready to give up everything and we know that. now you are pretending that you are suddenly on the good side. maybe you should have joined us a bit earlier on instead of not even participating. secondly, if you want to distinguish between the good and the bad you need to take indirect land use change into consideration, because that is the key point. if we do not take indirect land use change into consideration, you do not know which is good and which is bad. that is why i am advocating taking indirect land use change into account. you did not even want that. so you are the problem, in that you just want one target, stimulating agriculture instead of only stimulating the good biofuels. as to your third question, if i were a producer of second-generation biofuels at this moment and considering this deal i would not put my money in europe because this non-binding ... the commission has already shown that biofuels are a controversial subject and the long negotiations indicate that there were many obstacles to overcome. i would also like to thank the rapporteur for that. in the long term, we must always strive to use our resources more efficiently. it's sustainable and economical. our farmers and gardeners are crucial to this. they produce more and more food with less and less environmental impact. innovation has contributed to an increasingly effective and efficient use of raw materials. biofuels are an opportunity for farmers to make the most of a natural cycle. they can contribute to a more circular economy. for this we need the right and clear objectives and there must be room for innovation and experimentation. european agriculture is capable of great achievements because of constantly new insights. the agricultural sector is a major contributor to our common challenge.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, even in the most remote places of this planet, hardly any seabird is being found without plastic in its stomach. this is plastic that is not being digested, plastic that is often toxic. we all know that plastic bags are one of the major causes of this huge plastic pollution, and many of these birds, fish, turtles and other marine animals have fragments of the 100 billion european plastic bags that we use annually in their stomachs. but despite these devastating figures there is good news. actually, there are two parts this. first of all, this huge environmental problem is relatively easy to solve and, secondly, there is huge public support for the measures to solve this problem. therefore, the result that we are discussing today is, i think, an example of very good european policy-making. we have seen a strongly united european parliament, thanks to a very good rapporteur, ms auken – whom i thank – but also because we had a presidency, italy, that was not just focusing on avoiding problems between member states but that actually wanted to solve a problem, which is, unfortunately, something rather unique from the council side. contrary to what some people within the commission might think – and i do not mean you, commissioner vella – this legislation will be seen in the future as one of the european union’s best achievements of the last couple of years.

on behalf of the verts/ale group.– madam president, firstly i would like to extend my gratitude to the rapporteur, mr faria, for his excellent work on this file. mr faria, you had to take over the work of mr skylakakis and i think you did so very well, so thank you for that. my group can also support the deal which is on the table now. of course, at the moment we are only discussing the monitoring of international shipping, and we all know that international shipping is one of the few sectors which is not regulated for co2 at all. in that sense, a first step of monitoring and reporting on the co2 impact is of course a good start, but it is only a start. i am also very happy that, in the deal we have, there is an obligation for the commission to have biannual reporting on the full climate impact: shipping is more than just co2, and it is good that the commission will also report on that biannually. the most important question is what will the next step be, and that is a question for the commissioner, mr arias cañete. we all know that the imo is supposedly working on something, but we all know that this work is going very, very slowly, so my question to the commissioner would be: what are you going to do to make sure that the imo delivers on international shipping, ensures that they really do start reducing their emissions; and if they do not, then what is the commission planning to do about reducing the co2 emissions of shipping? we need to act, and just waiting for the imo to decide to do something might be too little, so i am curious and anxious for your answer. blue-card answer.– thank you for your question. i think i could not agree more that we should be doing something. maybe this also refers to mrs girling, who talked about the failure of international aviation. i see that completely differently because, by action at the european level, this also made the international world move on aviation. so sometimes you see a positive impact when europe moves, and in that sense indeed i fully agree with you that on shipping it is also time to act.
in writing. ‒ the abolition of the free bag reduces the amount of plastic waste that ends up in the environment. it is good that the european parliament has today supported measures to reduce the use of so-called 'lightweight plastic bags'. this is the case with the free bags that are often provided at the checkout counter for single use. the party for the animals has been advocating for the abolition of disposable carrier bags for some time. each year, some 100 billion plastic bags are used in europe, of which some 8 billion end up in the environment and our seas. the plastic carrier bag has an average life of only 15 minutes. bag waste is a typical example of waste that can be prevented. a price incentive is used to encourage people to use reusable bags. the proposal aims to reduce the use of plastic bags by 50 to 80% in the coming years. the remaining bags should also be made of more environmentally friendly and biodegradable materials.
in writing. ‒ this report deals with the new eu strategy for forests and the forest-based sector. however, in this report there are several environmental concerns that are not included in the text, like the biodiversity problems and the multifunctional nature of the forest. furthermore, the report advocates using forestry and forestry resources for biomass production by the energy industries, which then should be an important part for a future eu energy mix. for these reasons, the party for the animals cannot vote in favour here, therefore i have abstained on the final vote.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, firstly i note that, as usual, the council is absent. but then they do not have to be here because they probably already read about the document, that was adopted 30 minutes ago, last weekend in the german newspaper die welt where the commission was extensively quoted. my group thinks that there are very positive elements in the paper – such as the focus on better information sharing and the importance of fundamental rights and data protection – but unfortunately the paper is short on concrete proposals. one glaring omission in this paper is the evaluation of existing measures. commissioner timmermans referred to it, but where is the evaluation? maybe this will also be in die welt next weekend – i can't wait. so rather than proposing a whole raft of new measures, we should start with evaluating the existing toolkit, because you are the champion of better lawmaking, and i think evaluation is a part of better lawmaking. proposals to expand the role of europol are certainly interesting, but setting up this whole new structure must be done in a proper parliamentary process in the context of the current revision of the europol statute, and not sneakily via the back door of a mere commission communication. the revision of the transparency regulation is long overdue, but the commission and the absent member states stubbornly defend the old culture of secrecy, deliberately keeping citizens at a distance and refusing accountability. so i hope you are going to include this in your paper as well. my group is also worried about the increasingly blurred lines between policing and intelligence and security tasks, and the inflationary use of the term ‘national security’, without any proper legal definition. the proposals in the package also create a strange hybrid of national and european powers that leave citizens in the dark as to the applicable law. the proposals further weaken checks and balances, the very core of the democratic rule of law. alde firmly supports the achievement of a fully-fledged european area of freedom, security and justice, but always based on the principle of no new powers without more safeguards.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: let me remind you that in the recent terrorist attacks, the terrorists were known and followed by the police up to a certain point and then not anymore. instead of re-evaluating old-fashioned police investigative work, the european commission seems to want to store more data on flights. that doesn't seem like a productive way to go. in fact, i think that is dangerous because it distracts attention from following individuals who are suspected. to return to pnr (passenger name record) when the european parliament has been asking for years for evidence to show that it is helping is not the way to go. then judicial cooperation. yes, of course there will have to be more cooperation between member states in tracking down terrorists and criminals. but this also involves a solution to the problem of "trust", because so far european member states do not trust each other. we've also seen what happens to suspects who get caught between a rock and a hard place. my question to the commission is therefore: do you have any plans to improve that? cyber security, you know. how does the european commission view the right of individuals to protect themselves against government surveillance? after the snowden revelations and the nsa case, it's clear that you need to protect yourself more. but that means that member states may be less able to keep a close eye on their own citizens. i have the impression that it is not appreciated when citizens take better care of themselves. finally, mr president, more european internal security means more cooperation. but that requires more democratic control at european level. does the european commission also have a proposal on how we can increase the democratic content of our security policy?
we are talking today about security in europe and the commission's new security agenda. we all agree that security and the protection of fundamental rights must go hand in hand. there's a lot of attention here and in the media for the european jihadists, but what about the homeless, radicalized young people who don't travel to is territory but can be just as dangerous? is the commission paying attention to this group? i welcome the commission's emphasis on prevention and countering radicalisation. this is gonna take a lot more energy. in addition, potential terrorists are often already known to the intelligence services. the focus should therefore be on better tracking of these suspects. instead of collecting more and more data, the main objective is to improve cooperation between member states, with more and better information sharing. finally, an open democratic society must also be protected from extremists who do not share our values. i therefore ask the commission whether it is prepared to come forward with a proposal for a common european entry ban on hate preachers coming from outside europe to radicalise our young people in our cities.
we pay at the request of brussels, we cut at the request of brussels and we will soon be prosecuting at the request of brussels. to realise the euro-friendly dream of a united states of europe, you need not only a european flag, a european currency and a european anthem, but also a european public prosecutor's office. the netherlands and ten other countries drew a yellow card and got a middle finger back from brussels. and before the european public prosecutor's office is established, a debate is being launched on the extension of powers. we must not only relinquish the prosecution of fraud to the eu, but in the future also the prosecution of terrorism, child abduction and other crossborder crime. investigation and prosecution is a national matter. the eu has no place in this area. this isn't about tackling fraud or fighting terrorism, this is about power. we are being rolled into the united states of europe, where there is no place for the nation state. i hope my colleagues will bear this in mind when they vote on this report tomorrow.

i too would like to thank the court of auditors for the annual report, especially as the court of auditors has identified a fundamental problem in the european budget. it is far too little focused on results. the court of auditors states that the member states use a method they call 'use it or lose it'. the possible results of expenditure are of little importance. i will give you two examples of special reports from the court of auditors in the last year in which expenditure was found to be without demonstrable results. the first is the food processing industry programme, with a total budget of eur 5.6 billion, and the second is the programme for nature-friendly measures under the rural development fund: the commission has already over 16 billion in total over the seven-year budget period. i can't believe this! so much money and no results. we must therefore tackle non-results with the utmost urgency. a fundamental change in the european budget is badly needed. i am therefore pleased that my idea of setting up a special advisory group to this end has been accepted by the committee on budgetary control.

in the year 832, the then caliph of baghdad visited egypt and there he ordered his soldiers to demolish the great pyramid of giza. after months of effort, his soldiers still had little visible result. attempts to demolish this pre-islamic monument were therefore halted. this illustrates that islam is trying to erase all visible memory of what preceded it. islam has always wanted that, not just now in northern iraq and northern syria. genocide is the denial of genocide, and jihad is the erasure of all memories of the victims of jihad. i therefore ask the commission and anyone who hears or reads this not only to remember those victims but also to do everything in their power to ensure that europe is spared the same fate, because the enemy is already at our gates.
on behalf of the alde group.– madam president, last month former president nasheed, the first democratically-elected president of the maldives, was sentenced to 13 years in prison. charges of inciting terrorism, which not many people find credible, indicate that this trial is politically motivated. we call on the maldivian authorities to ensure that the appeals procedure meets free and fair trial standards and that former president nasheed gets access to a lawyer, access to doctors when needed, and has access to the advisers and lawyers he needs. the maldives are seeing a broader, worrying pattern of human rights violations. we have seen the disappearance of journalist ahmed rilwan, which is still unsolved, and the ban on peaceful protests is very troubling. the rise of jihadists and is recruitment in the maldives – in a disproportionately high number – is also very troubling to us. and as many european tourists enjoy their vacations on the bounty islands of the maldives, we as the eu call for them to be warned of the possible risk and the risk of instability in these islands. we hope that stability, the rule of law and due process, and fair trial for the former president are immediately restored.

on behalf of the verts/ale group.– madam president, i know that my colleague’s report is not about mr juncker’s investment plan, but i would like to touch on one issue which is really important for parliament: it is about the additionality of new projects of the juncker investment plan. to our great surprise, we saw the press release of last week, indeed of 22 april. if you look at the four projects, is this now really additionality? is this really ‘no more business as usual’? is this really the innovative europe that the juncker investment plan should be working on? you admitted yourselves that these projects would have got the money anyhow and that these projects were not indeed new. we are talking about the enlargement of an airport in croatia and about the green economy, but still the commission is saying these are examples of the juncker investment plan. well, this stresses the need for parliament to be far more clear on additionality in the negotiations because, otherwise, it is just business as usual.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to play a leading role in the world. we produce high quality food and meet the strictest food safety, environmental and animal welfare standards. a rapidly growing world population is raising the demand for food. agricultural land and raw materials, on the other hand, are becoming scarcer. so we have to produce more with less. with our innovations and the entrepreneurship of our farmers and gardeners, we can meet this challenge. legislation and regulation should encourage this, not inhibit it. too often we see regulations that do not correspond to practice. a good example is the innovation to make animal manure into a mineral concentrate that can be used as fertilizer. it is incomprehensible that the european commission should stand in the way of this sustainable innovation. the world is coming together at the milan world's fair. so it's a great opportunity to show the world what our farmers and gardeners are capable of.
food for the planet, energy for life. with such a theme, the milan world's fair is raising high hopes for sustainability and food security. but this event is in danger of being hijacked by big companies like coca cola and mcdonalds. these food giants benefit from overproduction and are more concerned about their own market position than about food distribution. the resolution does not mention that. it focuses not only on sustainability but also on increasing and intensifying food production. but that food production is already very high. we're already supplying more food than the world needs. the earth provides enough for everyone's needs. yet, a billion people go to bed hungry every day. one billion people! how could you do that? where does all this food go? well, we're throwing some of that food away. and part of that is making biofuels. but most of our food is fed to animals in the livestock industry. 70 percent of our land is used for animal feed production. and if we gave that to people, we could easily feed four billion more people. we do not need to increase or intensify food production. we need a transition to sustainable, fairly produced local food, and especially a transition from animal to plant food. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
for the ecr group the xylella bacteria has a serious impact on olive trees and has been spreading since 2013. in that year, efsa wrote, among other things: "to be effective, containment should include removal of infected plants". my first question to the commissioner is: has this measure been implemented in time to prevent spread? with regard to international trade: the eu import system is working well. plants are allowed to cross the border unless they're carrying a disease. specific risks may require increased controls. so that's risk-based. i would like to ask the commissioner specifically to continue to apply a risk-based approach in the future.
every farmer knows how terrible it is when your crops or livestock fall ill out of your control. the xylella outbreak has led to the closure of over 200 plant species yesterday. this is a huge financial loss. the italian government's decision not to enforce european legislation rigorously was a bad choice in terms of the consequences. firm and targeted controls are a better solution. the current rules must be strictly observed. i call on the european commission to provide more clarity in writing as soon as possible on the duration and scope of the import ban. i would also like to know the reasons why the european commission is considering a default decision against italy. finally, i am curious to see how the commissioner for better regulation will ensure that joint agreements are also unambiguously respected. europe is a trading powerhouse. incidents such as these should not be an excuse to change the current open import system.
i share the concerns of my colleagues from france, spain and italy. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the work of the european parliament. recently, the port of rotterdam has been repeatedly singled out in the media as a transit port for the dangerous bacterium. the netherlands is the only member state to have been on high alert for xylella following the 2013 outbreak in italy. the point is that the current rules should be better observed. i do not think that stricter rules in the form of a blockade for the internal market are a wise move at all, because the same market is of great importance for the agricultural and horticultural sector in europe, including countries not affected by xylella. the financial implications could be huge. if we move to a closed import system, the research costs of allowing certain plant and tree species onto the european market could be as high as eur 50 000 per species, and we certainly do not want that.
in writing. ‒ this resolution mandates the european commission to intervene in depth in the internal affairs of bangladesh. as regrettable as the situation in bangladesh is, it is up to bangladesh itself to shape its social and economic system. the eu is not about that. it is also up to the member states to decide which imported products they allow on the market and what requirements they impose as regards origin and method of production. the pvv therefore votes against.
in writing. this motion for a resolution will only lead to more illegal immigrants venturing across the mediterranean. by strengthening search operations at sea and thereby increasing the suction effect, the authors of this resolution will achieve the exact opposite of what they are aiming for, namely more deaths at sea. the pvv believes that this flow of illegal immigration must be stopped. the eu must therefore stop facilitating human trafficking and encouraging illegal immigration and instead make clear that the mediterranean shuttle service has come to an end. the focus should be on returning illegal immigrants to their countries of origin or departure, and on providing them with shelter in the region. this will of course put an end to the flow of illegal immigrants and the tragic incidents at sea. australia has already proven that this approach works.
the vvd fully recognises the humanitarian drama unfolding in the mediterranean. all drowning people must be rescued. the vvd is not convinced, however, that the measures proposed in the resolution will be able to reduce or prevent such tragedies in the future. the focus on the reception of refugees in the region is lacking and no measures are proposed to prevent the departure of refugees from the libyan coast to the eu. the vvd considers the provision of humanitarian visas to eu embassies to be wrong in view of the sucking effect, and is against the imposition of refugee quotas on member states from brussels.
in writing. ‒ the drowning deaths of so many refugees and migrants in recent weeks and months is appalling. europe cannot allow this to happen. i am in favour of a substantial financial, operational and territorial extension of triton, with contributions from all the member states and the european commission. i am also in favour of greater cooperation between member states and eu agencies and a partial extension of the responsibilities of these agencies. at the same time, the situation in the source countries must be improved and the reception in the region improved, for example with winter and summer tents. member states can also resettle more refugees currently staying in un camps in the region. it is also crucial to tackle the criminal traffickers, including through prosecution, financial and other sanctions, and the confiscation and destruction of their assets. as regards the issue of humanitarian visas, member states should be free to decide whether or not to issue them. as regards the necessary partial redistribution of asylum seekers among the member states, the council should take the lead, not the commission.
in writing. ‒ the european union has granted trade advantages to bosnia and herzegovina in the past in order to export to the eu at a profit. the european commission is now proposing to allow these trade advantages to be suspended if bosnia and herzegovina is guilty of violations of the fundamental principles of human rights, democracy and the rule of law. it is good that europe is putting these human rights ahead of commercial interests. the proposal also calls for the accession of bosnia and herzegovina to the eu. enlargement of the european union is unwise as long as it does not function democratically. the party for the animals is opposed to the accession of new member states in the present circumstances and cannot therefore agree with the commission's proposal. in order not to block the respect of human rights, abstention from voting was chosen.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, we strongly condemn this week’s cruel executions by firing squad of eight people in indonesia, seven of whom were not even indonesian citizens. not only has the european union, along with australia, strongly appealed to the authorities not to go ahead with the executions, the eu also offered assistance to the indonesian authorities in tackling drug trafficking. punishing convicted criminals can be done in more humane ways and must always be preceded by a fair trial. we call for a halt to all pending executions in indonesia. colleagues, it is quite ironic, and actually shameful that on the day that we heard the appalling news of indonesia’s executions, viktor orbán, whose party still remains a member of the ppe group, suggested that the death penalty would be reinstated in hungary. such proposals are entirely inappropriate. as mr orbán knows very well, the death penalty is banned in europe. i think we should be proud of that, as well as of our international efforts to end the death penalty, to limit its application and to seek countries putting a moratorium on this cruel and inhumane punishment. we have set the bar, as europe, and intend to keep it rising, as liberal democracies. it would strengthen the voice of democracies in making appeals to countries such as indonesia if the united states would stand side by side with europe and would also outlaw the death penalty. we will – and we must – continue our efforts globally by working with governments and civil society organisations to ensure that the death penalty will soon belong to the past.
