the rapporteur. - tomorrow we shall be voting on crucial legislation: the regulation on benchmarks used in financial instruments and financial contracts. the last few months have been a blast. investigations, billions in fines, headlines about fraud and embezzlement in the financial sector. the reason this time: the 4pm fixed benchmark, a benchmark price on which exchange rates of all major currencies are based. and the libor scandal also brought another record fine, this time for deutsche bank. the myth that benchmark fraud is the work of a few bad apples within a few banks has been finally broken. manipulation has occurred and is occurring in all sectors, including large and important benchmarks. all of this demonstrates the importance of benchmark indices for financial stability and also the need for good regulation to prevent manipulation of these types of systemically important benchmarks in the future. the legislation we are voting on tomorrow is designed to protect the stability of the financial system and the real economy. this means that there must be sufficient safeguards for large benchmarks such as libor or the benchmarks for currency exchange that we all use on a daily basis. but the rules must also ensure that a wide and diverse range of indices remains possible, both within and outside europe. indeed, a large supply allows investors to switch and switch to other benchmarks. without alternatives, more and more instruments and contracts will be linked to a shrinking set of indices, resulting in greater market concentration, more power for the producers of the big indices and, of course, greater risks to the financial system and the economy. competition in the benchmark industry fosters innovation, reduces systemic risk and enhances market discipline. this means that there should be a balanced treatment in the regulation for smaller benchmarks. the report, as it stands, offers that balance, a balance between large and small benchmarks, between financial and commodity benchmarks, and between those benchmarks produced in the eu and outside. that balance is badly needed to protect consumers, to grow capital markets, to promote economic recovery and also to achieve the vision and goals of a european capital markets union. i think that is also why the economic affairs committee supported the text with a majority of 48 votes in favour and only 10 against. fortunately, that support came from parties on both the left and the right. i would like to thank my fellow negotiators, the shadow rapporteurs, very warmly for the collegial and constructive way in which they contributed to the preparation of the report. i very much look forward to continuing that cooperation in a good way during the trialogue. i call on all the groups to support this report so that we can move quickly and with a strong mandate into these trialogue discussions so that after almost two years of discussion we can actually have good benchmark legislation in place by the end of this year.
i would like to thank all the speakers for their input and their valuable contribution today. i'm very pleased with the broad support. of course, we have not been able to make everyone 100% happy, but i am glad that the report as a whole is widely supported by all of you. i would like to welcome the expressions of appreciation and the compliments, but i would also like to share them with my colleague, koen ter wal, who has put a lot of hard work into this. i should also like to mention the policy adviser to the alde group, petra, and the people in the secretariat of the committee on economic affairs. because this was my first report and it is quite a search to go through all the procedures in this parliament. so i'm also very grateful to everyone who has contributed in that way. i would like to say to mr monot that he is very wrong in thinking that the critical benchmarks are only libor and euribor, because that is not the case. it is not for nothing that we have added qualitative criteria to the quantitative criteria, so that there must always be an evaluation to see whether something is a critical benchmark or not. i shall not go into the substance of the matter, because that would take too long. i very much look forward to the trialogue with the shadow rapporteurs and, of course, with the commission and the council, in order to take those important steps towards proper regulation of benchmarks as soon as possible.
development aid is not about taking from the rich and giving to the poor: it's taking from the poor and giving to the rich. the most striking example is the 234 million euro transferred by the european commission to mr mugabe. mr mugabe who then, as a token of appreciation, had two elephants slaughtered and a large barbecue. this 234 million euros is hard earned tax money. money that people in the netherlands and the rest of europe have to work hard for. these people pay that tax on the assumption that it is being handled with care. but what is the european commission doing? who gives this hard-earned money to a corrupt african dictator! how hard can it be to get along with people who pay taxes?
on behalf of the gue/ngl group, i would like to thank the commissioner for her statement. i think we all agree that cigarette smuggling and counterfeit cigarettes must be tackled. the problem is that the cooperation with the cigarette manufacturers originally arose from a legal settlement. we are now many years later and it has become clear that the tobacco industry is continuing its lobbying, including against the tobacco directive. that a lawsuit is even being brought against the european commission over the tobacco directive. they're not exactly friendly guys, i'd say. they are also no longer the guys that the international community, with the world health organization at the forefront, thinks you should treat like any other lobbyist or corporation. we have strict rules: if i receive a tobacco lobbyist in my office, i must not only tell him that i had that contact, but also tell him exactly what it was about, what material i received. we are asking the commission to do exactly the same thing. you have contacts with the tobacco industry - extremely sensitive - and you are obliged to give us all the material, not just to us members of parliament, but to the people, that the tobacco industry gives you. i would like the commissioner to give me his assurance that the people of europe will have access to this material.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i think it is a good sign that we are having this debate with the commissioner in charge of the digital market, although i am wondering who is really in charge, because i would say that this is a taxation matter. i have been doing a bit of digging, and sometimes that gives you funny results. for example, commissioner, one of the first questions relating to the application of the higher vat rate to e-books was introduced by mrs thyssen, who was then a member of parliament and who is now actually your colleague. mrs thyssen was very worried about the higher vat rates on, i quote, ʽbooks published on cd-rom or dvdʼ. now that was in 2009 – the commission has not even started to think about solutions but cd-roms are no longer used for e-books. so the key words here are speed and urgency. because i have also submitted a series of questions over the years, and in the answer to one of my questions in 2013 the commission replied that it ʽis currently examining this issueʼ – blah di blah di blah – ʽand these elements will be taken on board in completing the impact...ʼ. in other words, already back in 2013 the commission was looking into it. so what are you waiting for? the digital market, as you well know commissioner, is developing much more rapidly than this. we are legislating at flintstone speed but the digital market is moving on. it is not waiting for us, and if you say 2016, well by then so many small businesses will have gone down already because they cannot survive. why does the commission have to wait until 2016? why cannot you just start tomorrow? if, commissioner, you go back to the commission and to the member states and tell them that we need action now, i think that the whole parliament will back you up because everybody here is echoing the same message. so i would say, commissioner, let us get to work tomorrow.
the european parliament has also called for the eu to be involved in the fight against terrorism. they may not start it. they may start out because of ideological differences, but they often go on for longer because of raw materials. governments and rebels fund themselves, and that brings us to the chicken or the egg question: is the raw material the cause of the war or is the war caused by the raw material? what is clear is that commodities finance wars. if you take that away, and we've done that before, you take away the funding and you might stop the war. think of what we've done to the garden furniture. hardwood is currently certified, not voluntarily, but compulsorily. think of what we've done to the diamond trade. that is not a voluntary arrangement. that's a mandatory policy. we have seen in recent years that voluntary rules are never respected. european companies have been able to comply with the oecd guidelines for the certification of products containing conflict minerals since 2011. they're not doing it! the commission's proposal now on the table, voluntary self-certification, will therefore have the same shortcoming as all other voluntary systems. the commission's proposal on international trade limits it to only 20 european smelters and refiners and does not take the large quantities. you all have phones. you have it all laid out for you, you work with it every day. there are no phones that are made in europe. they're all made in china. this type of device will not be covered by european legislation and that means that no consumer can ask: where did this stuff come from? and the chinese companies will not have to abide by our european rules. this is about war in the congo, about women being raped, about children working in the mines. we can stop that. this is not about the german automobile industry and their problems with bureaucracy!
mr president, it is essential that trade policies are a key element in broader eu foreign policy goals, such as conflict resolution, human rights and development. that is why we need effective measures to break the link between the world’s most terrible conflicts and the role the trade in minerals plays in them; a link that currently leads from war to our living rooms. we must make a clear distinction between legitimate mining and trading in blood-stained minerals globally. for our group, the commission proposals – which were mostly voluntary measures – did not go far enough. we put forward amendments with mandatory schemes for smelters and refiners, which are the key players in this market and cover most of the global market. we also called for support mechanisms for small and medium-sized enterprises in their efforts to ensure due diligence and compliance, because consumers deserve transparency. through a review, we ensure that adequate enough mechanisms are in place to make sure that we accomplished in practice what we hope to accomplish now with this regulation. there are important lessons to be learned from the past, such as from the dodd-frank act which unintentionally led to congo-free instead of conflict-free implications and hurt many people in their legitimate work and livelihoods. we should not hurt people with our good intentions. so meanwhile, while we focus on these important trade measures, let us look at the bigger picture too and keep an eye on all measures that are needed to end conflict all over the world, especially in the great lakes regions.
i am sorry to have to say that i have not been able to make a reply to the question. there will be many opportunities to talk to the commissioner about the individual proposals and we will be happy to do so. my group will pay particular attention to the interests of consumers and the interests of smes. i would like to give you some more general considerations in this debate today. then i look back to the first time i went online myself. it was a whole new world, it was an open world and a safe haven. i was able to connect with people in other parts of the world. i could make my own website. i could be creative on the internet. now we're 20 years ahead. and if i look at what's happening now, we have governments on one side, intelligence agencies that want to know a lot of us and don't want the internet to be so free. we also see big companies. we know the commission is going after google. but we also have companies like amazon, facebook. it's becoming more and more the internet of the multinationals. i hear - and i agree - that innovation is of great importance to the european economy. and that includes the internet. but then the internet must remain the place for creativity and innovation, and that can only happen when the internet is free and open. i respect the commission's action against google. but keep going, take on the other multinationals, and make sure that the internet stays open to small businesses, to innovation, and to people, so that we can continue to be creative with each other.

rapporteur.– madam president, thank you to my co-rapporteur, mr kariņš, to the shadows who have helped us in this term and in the previous term, to the chair of the committee on economic and monetary affairs, roberto gualtieri, and thanks to the council, particularly the italians, and the commission. it has been a very long process. we started in the previous term and we managed to conclude last december, and let me remind you that we had a plenary vote before the elections that gave mr kariņš and myself a mandate that was very strong: 643 votes in favour of a public register for ultimate beneficiary owners and 30 against. it gave us a mandate that did not allow us to backtrack, and that i really appreciate. we acted on that and my motivation has always been to deal with tax avoidance and tax evasion. we had thabo mbeki visiting this house and he said that the amount of money fleeing out of africa because multinationals are not paying their taxes in the country where the work is done is immense. we had the scandal with the son of the former president of the ukraine, who put the money he stole from the ukrainian people into a shell company in the netherlands and then we had luxleaks. that helped to clarify why we are doing this. the fatf and the commission made tax offences a predicate offence, which is good, which helped us work, and then we created this public register on ultimate beneficiary owners. it is article 30. we agreed to have 28 registers. there are countries that do not have an ultimate beneficiary ownership register at the moment and they are now obliged to have one. in future, these 28 registers will be interconnected, allowing people to search from shell company to shell company, from country to country. who can look at these registers? well the authorities, of course, they have full access, the obligatory entities – banks, casinos, etc. – but also anybody who has a legitimate interest, anybody who can show they have an interest in looking at them because of money-laundering, terrorism and issues like corruption, tax crime and fraud. so in the near future journalists and ngos and every citizen in europe will be able to look at the register and make sure that we collectively clean up our act, that we do not allow shell companies to steer away funding, to steer away tax money that can be used for education, for health, for everything we need in our society. i am proud of that, and if member states want to go further than that, if they want to add more causes, they can, and i think they should. we cleaned up the data protection and may i thank commissioner jourová, because as soon as the file moved from dg markt to dg justice we got a better hearing on the issue of data protection. so we made sure that the data subject, the person who wants to know something – perhaps they have a feeling that something went wrong with their data – has rights to redress, without tipping off criminals, but they have the right to redress. we respect data retention rules. data retention is set at five years and i am happy to have that. that is article 40. so let us make this an example for other countries in the world. would it not be fantastic if the usa were to follow us, would it not be marvellous in our fight against tax avoidance and tax evasion? i want everyone to help us reach that.
money laundering costs the global economy €1.3 trillion a year, a huge sum of money obtained illegally, for example, through human smuggling and drug trafficking. and that's now being brought into the mainstream economy. i think that's unfair. this new legislation will help us to tackle money laundering and terrorist financing by making economic ownership and risks clearer. i'm with you on that. that is good for fair trade. tackling fraudulent practices also does justice to businessmen and other law-abiding citizens, as they should. one note on data protection. can personal data obtained for money laundering be used for other purposes, for example commercial? i would like to hear the commissioner's answer to that, because she really has to keep her finger on the pulse here. wash whistle-blowers' ears, but make sure citizens and their data are well protected.
rapporteur.– mr president, many of my colleagues pointed out that the public register would not be ‘public’ because you have to have a legitimate interest. i would like to point out that we made sure that in the proposal it says that the authority has to clarify whether you do or not, so they have to prove that you do not have a legitimate interest. i would like the first journalist who has been told he or she does not have a legitimate interest to go to court, clarify it and, at the same time, open up the public register and make it even more public. i do not think any authority can say that you, as a citizen, do not have a legitimate interest to look into a register to look for fraud, corruption and anti-money laundering issues. you will get that access. it is also a self-fulfilling prophecy: if we start saying here that it has its limitations, it will have its limitations. if we say here, ‘let us try it out’, we will open up that register even more.
the question of a maternity leave directive has been discussed for years. it is not just the commission that is trying to impose the law on the member states, but also the european parliament, which is happy to dictate. the debate on the right to 20 weeks, 16 weeks or perhaps 40 weeks of leave should take place in national parliaments, not in this parliament. this directive is a typical example of unnecessary european interference and interference in national affairs. the commission promised fewer rules and less interference through mrs timmermans. a lot of yelling and a little wool. i call on the commission to honour its promise, to withdraw the proposal and above all not to come forward with a new proposal. sovereign states can very well set their own rules that take into account the interests of men, women and children. the resolution on the table today can therefore, as far as i am concerned, be thrown out of the window.
we all know that the first weeks after the birth of a child are crucial. during these precocious weeks, the extent to which the father can help in the household and whether the mother can return to full-time employment is determined. a well-regulated maternity leave is crucial for the whole life of the father, the mother and the child, and therefore also for our ageing economy and the competitiveness of the future. it is therefore very disappointing to see that the council sees pregnant women as a mere cost item and that the commission simply wants to withdraw the proposal without taking account of the judgment of the european court of justice. we in parliament have shown flexibility and a willingness to reach a common position in good consultation. we therefore ask the council and the commission to meet us there. unilateral withdrawal is not a credible option in this case. europe, as the founder of the principle of equal pay for equal work, must really give women the chance to catch up.
the european commission is proud to be fighting with the council to distribute refugees fairly across europe and i am therefore saddened that one member state after another is saying that it does not want to do so. the only approach which seems to be shared by the council is an approach of military action, of more border controls and of sending more people back. i heard you yesterday, commissioner timmermans, telling stories about the hungarian refugees who were welcomed with open arms in other european countries in 1956. those open arms, where are they now? they are not in the council, but they are not in you, commissioner, nor in the commission. the dublin revision is not in the proposals, and that is what you wanted so badly, mr avramopoulos. i thought we were going to see what we were doing to dublin. i can't find it back. i think there is a difference of opinion in the commission and that is why you are not making your proposals. you write in your own agenda that europe will be facing a demographic deficit in the near future. but you leave it at that. do i have to fill it out myself? an old continent surrounded by countries with young populations cannot limit itself to fighting illegal immigration by tightening controls. a rich continent surrounded by countries at war or in poverty cannot afford that. if there is work for low-skilled or low-paid workers then you will have to facilitate that too, because otherwise you will see illegality and illegal work increase. in the previous period we saw the directive on seasonal work. we should have built on that now. what about the proposals to facilitate the palestinian nannies, ladies who work for us illegally and no one else wants to do it? what about the circular migration proposals? mr. chairman, you can make a schedule for a week. you can make a calendar for a few months. you can make a schedule until the sea is rough again and the refugees stay on the other side for a while. but i expect the european commission to set out an agenda for the coming years, for the coming generation. and that, mr president, is not there. (the speaker will reply to a question on a blue card in accordance with rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure.)
"blue card" is the answer. i am happy to accept the invitation to visit a refugee camp in the czech republic. i greatly appreciate it from any country that takes in refugees. i do think that we are all still not doing enough. the resettlement of 20,000 people from refugee camps is a good thing that the member states do not want to do. but it's only a tiny bit. i would also like to say, and this is the last point, that there is a great deal of variation between countries in the way people are taken in, given status and given a real new life.
today we're going to discuss the immigration agenda. a first-class piece of shit. we have to redistribute the thousands of illegal immigrants. each country will be given a proportionate share of the number of illegal immigrants and terrorists. so if you think they will be affected by quotas, you have really misunderstood. they will move freely, for our borders are gone. and with the increasing flow of illegal immigrants, the dangers are only getting greater. not for these lucky people, for they are the ones for whom we are extending the rescue missions, but for the citizens of the european union. if you think it can't get any more idiotic, there's always mrs. mogherini: no refugees or migrants intercepted at sea will be sent back against their will. she said yesterday. the smugglers are the laughing parties, because they make you rich. and not to mention the islamic state terrorists who are travelling among the illegal immigrants and will sow death and destruction in the european union. the losers are the people of the european countries. our borders must be secure, watertight. real border patrol, like they do in australia. they send back the illegal immigrants, and they don't drown people off the coast. that's where we're going.
we've started the day very badly. we have seen that the council welcomed a decision which amounts to the deployment of military personnel, whereas the deployment of military personnel in libya was part of the problem which is at the root of the flow of refugees. we have seen a commission that is happy with a migration agenda, but has no say in the broken dublin system. it is planned to resettle 20 000 people over two years. the un is asking for 130,000 resettlement places. on the whole, european asylum policy, including dublin, is a mess. we should have started to identify that early this morning and then we should have looked at what the un refugee agency's proposals are. no one has mentioned the un action plan here, which does offer solutions: the european union is also responsible for ensuring that the refugees are resettled en masse, that they have good humanitarian visas and other safe routes to seek asylum and receive shelter in the region. we must help those countries in the region. i'd like to hear that. the current policy is a disgrace.
last monday, eu defence and foreign ministers decided to set up a european mission in the mediterranean to tackle the criminal networks of human smugglers. the member states have thus shown that, where the political will is present, they can act decisively and with unity. however, the willingness to address the other aspects of this same refugee problem, such as creating safe, legal routes for people in need and organising solidarity within the eu, is unfortunately much less. at the beginning of this debate the council did not even dare to mention the term 'refugee' and spoke only of the need to stop illegal immigration. the commission rightly points out in its new migration agenda that the answer to this humanitarian crisis lies in an integrated approach. it is therefore time for the council to come to that conclusion and to start providing a safe haven for those who need it most.
in writing. ‒ conflict minerals must be banned from the european market. at the same time, the local population must not be made the victim of european legislation. that is why i support a self-certification system. i would also like to stress that such legislation is not the solution but only a tool. the eu must work on the world stage to resolve conflicts and support governments in implementing the necessary reforms.
in writing. ‒ mining of minerals that eu countries import often leads to violent conflict, human rights abuses and environmental damage. the package of measures adopted today demonstrates the ambition to prevent minerals from conflict zones. that's a clear signal. for example, european companies must report on the origin of minerals they use, so that electronics and mobile phones made from raw materials from conflict areas can be banned. this prevents violent groups in conflict zones from financing their weapons by selling raw materials produced in their region. there will also be mandatory certification for tin, tantalum, tungsten and gold, to ensure that they do not come from conflict zones. the party for the animals, however, believes that we must go further, with mandatory due diligence for all types of raw materials and not just these four. i also believe that all companies processing metals and minerals, and not just importers, both upstream and downstream in the supply chain, must demonstrate that their products are free of conflict minerals. i therefore abstained on the final vote on the commission proposal and i hope that parliament will manage to settle these matters in the negotiations with the council. only then will i agree fully.
in writing. ‒ while i support the intention of the resolution, maternity leave is primarily a national competence. that is why i am not in favour of new european rules in this area and i abstained.
the pvv voted today against the resolution calling for the council to reopen negotiations on the maternity leave directive. the pvv is not against extra days off for new fathers and extra rest for new mothers. we do not think, however, that this debate should be held in the european parliament, because social benefits are and must remain a national competence. it is therefore up to national governments to take decisions at the level of their own country.
on behalf of the s&d group.– madam president, my thanks to the council and commission for their contributions to this debate. the eu and turkey are important strategic partners and it is in the interests of both to have close and effective cooperation. we encourage turkey to accelerate the pace of negotiations by clearly committing itself to respecting democratic values and principles. parliament continues to support the eu accession process of turkey as the cornerstone of our relations. however, in the report which will be voted on tomorrow we asked the commission to reassess the way in which the negotiations with turkey have been conducted so far, and how things can be further improved and intensified. at a time when we ourselves are having intensive discussions in the eu – and indeed this morning in this chamber – about how adequate protection can be given to refugees, we commend and praise all the efforts made by the turkish government in hosting more refugees than any other country in the world today. if we want to make sure that those in need of protection can find a safe haven in the region, we need to step up our efforts actively to assist turkey in providing adequate shelter, healthcare and education for refugees and their children. another area in which we strongly support and encourage efforts by the turkish government and all other stakeholders is the drive to achieve a comprehensive and sustainable conclusion to the peace process with the kurdish community. but there is also clear concern in this parliament about certain developments in turkey. these are issues at the core of the negotiation process, such as the independence of the judiciary, freedom of expression and the freedom of the press. the pressure put on media outlets and journalists, which often results in the intimidation, dismissal or even arrest of journalists and widespread self-censorship, is of great concern to all of us. the recently adopted security package is, in our view, very wide in scope and bypasses the judiciary. most investigations into the events that occurred in gezi park in may and june 2013 are still pending, and too little progress has been made in identifying the alleged perpetrators. we believe that the eu should remain a benchmark for reforms in the country. that means that we do not want to shut the door to turkey, but we are calling for more dialogue and intensified engagement. the european parliament report provides several proposals in areas where we believe it is of mutual benefit to increase cooperation. these are foreign and security policy, anti-terrorism, energy security and also, of course, specifically, the field of fundamental rights. delivering the official opening benchmarks and a subsequent opening of chapters 23 and 24, which has just been referred to by the commission representative here, is the best way to promote truly effective reforms. we are ready to support turkey in developing solid democratic institutions and effective legislation based on respect for human rights, the rule of law and defence of the interests of all sectors of turkish society. finally, parliament also reiterates its strong support for the reunification of cyprus on the basis of a fair, comprehensive and viable settlement for both communities there. we very much welcome the fact that the leaders of both communities have officially resumed negotiations under the auspices of the un, and we believe that there is a unique window of opportunity now for a shared solution to the benefit of all cypriots. we expect that turkey and all relevant stakeholders will play a positive and constructive role in this delicate process. let me mention here my gratitude and my thanks to all the shadow rapporteurs on the turkey report, because if this had not been a joint exercise it would have been very difficult for me to stand here. i am happy that we have a united position on this here in parliament.
madam president, the annual progress report has sadly become a regress report. the concerns we have on the rule of law, separation of powers, human rights and fundamental freedoms are now even more substantial than in previous years as a result of the passing of security laws, restrictions on press and internet freedom, and an acknowledgment that evidence in a mass trial was actually doctored. that leads to key questions about fair trial and due process. i have always fought for the accession process as i believed it would lead to a win-win situation for the people in the eu and turkey alike, and i am sad to share how sombre i am about where things stand. now, are we only highlighting concerns? no. we also clearly stressed the importance of updating the customs union and of a common coordinated approach to foreign policy and humanitarian matters, but it must be crystal clear that an effective and credible eu—turkey partnership can only be based on solidly shared values and a common understanding of democratic principles, such as are laid down in the copenhagen criteria.
i was actually going to take one case to the european commission. a very serious matter. on 18 april 2007, a triple murder of christians took place in malatya, turkey, a horrific murder. a torture murder that was filmed by the suspects or the perpetrators, even with their phones. they were also arrested at the scene. now we're eight years later, and what do we find? these suspects are at large, much to the dismay and dismay of the relatives who were threatened during the hearings. it is also a shock to the members of the small turkish protestant community, who feel unsafe because of the intimidation by the perpetrators during the trial. they ask themselves: what is the right for christians in turkey? i called them again this afternoon to ask if there was any lead in this case. there have been 101 sessions. i would urge the commission to consult the turkish judicial authorities: how's this murder case coming along? the perpetrators are free and under so-called house arrest.
turkey has destroyed all progress since ataturk. christians, kurdish political opposition, journalists, judges, women are systematically oppressed with violence. the beast of ankara has given isis free rein in the battle for kobani, revealing its true agenda. turkey is heading towards an islamofascist dictatorship led by the dangerous islamist erdoğan. and what does the eu do? it rewards this misconduct. it feeds the beast that's gonna bite our throats off. it gives not less, but more money to dictator erdoğan, who has just spent 300 million on a megalomaniacal palace, 50 times bigger than the white house, in which he hangs the sultan. mr president, this country must never join the eu. i demand that this commission immediately stop all funding of this islamic dictatorship, give a detailed account of the spending of the billions it has received through and through corrupt turkey and immediately stop the negotiation process.
a lot has been said in this debate. on the one hand, criticism from the rapporteur, who has done a lot of work on this report. this is right, because in recent years and months turkey has done what is necessary and what is not in line with european values. the examples have already been given. but on the other hand, i also note that this rapporteur and this report want more than ever to open new chapters in the negotiations with turkey. let us not turn this around, ladies and gentlemen, because this will be seen in ankara as a reward and an encouragement, whereas as far as i am concerned there is no reason to rejoice and this is not the signal we want to send. does that mean i'm closing the door on turkey? no, i'm sure you didn't. we need to keep in touch with this important neighbor. we must continue to monitor progress on key benchmarks and, of course, continue to support the development of the rule of law from below. but perhaps we should dare to look each other in the eye in this parliament and in the european commission. because we have all entered a process, which is focused - as if we had blinders on - on membership and membership only. perhaps we can also come to the conclusion on both sides that it is better to invest - in a critical spirit - in an alternative partnership. it takes political courage to sometimes deviate from the beaten track and the pre-established standard route. the epp is in any case prepared to look at these alternatives in the same constructive spirit and i hope that other colleagues will also be inspired by this realism.
it's almost summer and it's almost eight years since the great financial crisis hit europe. banks were faltering, businesses were going under, and unemployment was skyrocketing. six million school leavers entered the labour market with poor prospects. youth unemployment is skyrocketing across europe. the european parliament has been committed to a new perspective for young people from the most affected regions. partly as a result of this effort, money is now available for pre-financing. programmes to help young people enter the labour market no longer have to wait for their national governments to provide co-financing. they don't have to waste time. but noblesse oblige, noblesse obliged. i'm not sure what you mean. in six months' time we will want to know whether we are on the right track, because we do not want an eighth generation of school leavers without prospects on the labour market.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group whales have been protected for 50 years, and an international ban on commercial whaling has been in place since the 1980s. and now, almost 30 years later, there are still countries that are not bothered by that ban, such as iceland, norway and japan. they hunt under false pretenses, killing 1,500 whales every year. and what has europe done to prevent these crimes? i don't know anything. it is time for europe to act. many species of whales are endangered and will go extinct if we don't do something now. besides, the whaling is downright horrible. japanese whalers chase the animals into the icy waters of the southern ocean and then harpoon them in the body. a long and gruesome death struggle ensues. then there's another point. in taiji, japan, many thousands of dolphins are hunted into a bay each year, herded together, and killed. a great slaughter is the result! i would welcome it if the council were to come up with a clear position, as the netherlands has already done in the fight against whaling. the whale hunt must stop and it can stop if europe puts pressure on japan through its trade policy. and then you're not going to negotiate a trade agreement, because countries that allow animals to go extinct to make money don't deserve support. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
it is indeed good news that the economy in the eurozone seems to be coming out of the doldrums. we're really going in the right direction. it's good that we see that the eurozone grew faster than the us economy in the first quarter. i am glad that the netherlands, the country from which i come, has an important role to play in this. but we're not there yet. i've heard it from colleagues. if we want to stabilise this, if we want to make real progress, then we really have to reform. and i think we can contribute to that. i would actually like to use the example of indian prime minister modi, because he said, as we have here: "we have to cut the red tape". the european commissioner frans timmermans has just presented his plans for this. i really hope he gets his act together and that this is a success. but prime minister modi added something to that. he said: "we have to replace the red tape with a red carpet for our businesses". and that red flag for our business, i don't see it so badly. we do have plans for an energy union, because energy prices are of course much higher than in other parts of the world. we really must make the single market work quickly. we are talking about a capital markets union, but we are still working on a green paper on that. we really need to try and get some speed on that. and what i 'm sure i 'll miss: the juncker plan talks about reindustrialisation and has remained deafeningly silent on this. so i would like to hear the commission's plans in the short term. which way are we going now? because if we want to solve that unemployment problem, the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the situation of the unemployed. so we really need to give the companies the space to create those jobs and we also need to make sure that we explain the red carpet to the companies. just good jobs for everyone in europe and worldwide we achieve in three ways. firstly, by ratifying and respecting worldwide the rights of workers as enshrined in the core ilo conventions. secondly, by sustainable international supply chains with compulsory corporate responsibility for what happens in their supply chain. and thirdly, by providing transparency to consumers by labelling products made under decent working conditions. these are the three issues that germany has put on the agenda for the g7 summit next week. my appeal is: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject. only through the right to association can workers bring about a positive change in their working conditions and only through collective bargaining can they guarantee themselves a fair wage. too often, it is a reality on paper and workers' rights are trampled underfoot. so it's more than just a voluntary action in the countries where many of our products are made. the g7 will be able to increase its legitimacy by using its economic power to bring about real economic change. it is only through tough international agreements that something is done for the girls in the garment factories in bangladesh and for the miners who extract the conflict minerals for us. it would be nice if commission president juncker and council president tusk would support this mandatory method.
it is always welcome if we want to make european regulation simpler and more effective. but sometimes it is better to ask ourselves whether we want certain regulations at all. european school milk and fruit is one such scheme. a few years ago the european court of auditors concluded that the added value of a european school milk and fruit scheme is nil. europe wants to be big in big things and small in small things. i would therefore ask the european commission to consider the added value of a school milk and fruit scheme. the national member states are perfectly capable of setting up such schemes themselves. it does not require costly and unnecessarily complicated bureaucracy in brussels. sometimes we have to bite through the sour apple and dare to abolish rules. this is a project that has gone off the rails, and i understand the history very well, but i don't see the future. it has no clear purpose or added value. i shall therefore vote against this report.
in writing. as far as the party for the animals is concerned, the school milk and school fruit programmes can be reduced to one programme, namely a school fruit and vegetables programme. the school milk program can be abolished. milk is not as healthy as we may think. recent scientific research from sweden confirms this. for example, drinking milk increases the risk of bone fractures. we have also heard several members of parliament today talking about lactose intolerance, which is becoming more common among young people. advocating "to counter the decline in milk consumption among adolescents", as some here do literally, is thus an attack on public health. but it is also an attack on nature, on the climate and on the animals, which are being kept in ever more massive stables. we shouldn't exploit animals and milk them. we could spend the school milk budget on more school fruits and vegetables. and by doing so, we're reducing the negative impact of intensive livestock farming on our planet and giving children healthier food choices. we also think it is inappropriate to have european logos everywhere. this form of propaganda is outrageous symbolism on the backs of children. i also believe that all european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.

the g7 has decided to spend 90 billion euros to combat the alleged global warming, promising that it will not exceed 2°c compared to 1990. it takes a lot of guts to call these kinds of hard numbers. you may wonder how they came up with these numbers and whether this amount and this promise are well founded. after all, this is a subject that we now know fiction and reality intertwine. the question is whether the earth is warming, and what is causing it, and it is entirely uncertain what measures might help. a rather shaky basis for drawing 90 billion euros. if you're so damn tough to call hard numbers, then have the balls to tell me who's gonna bring them up. and it is the taxpayer, the hardworking middle class in the netherlands and elsewhere, who will see their energy bill rise. that's a fairly accurate calculation. what is not exactly calculable is how much of that 90 billion will end up in the pockets of al gore's corporations.

madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur for the report. it is good that we started at 3 o’clock with the climate debate and are now having an energy security debate. maybe we could have merged those two discussions because, very clearly, energy security and climate protection can – and should – go hand in hand. therefore, if one really takes this seriously, it is a clear agenda for energy efficiency – the fastest, cheapest way of reducing our dependence on energy – and renewables. none of the other options have those advantages. maybe there is still someone asking about nuclear energy; well, i would say, go to finland or hungary where they are building nuclear – or want to build nuclear – and are now deeply dependent on russian technology and russian uranium. so that is no longer any help in terms of energy security. i have one question for the commissioner, to end once and for all the gossip: will you now make very clear that the eu will never ever again allow public money to go into nuclear? it would be very helpful in this debate if we closed that circus as well.
thank you for the rapporteur and shadow rapporteurs. europe is torn between a national energy mix, a desired climate policy and a heavy reliance on external suppliers of fossil fuels, often from countries with dubious regimes. this field of tension makes energy in europe expensive, polluting and also unsafe. now that we have set out the ambitions for a green, cheap and secure energy supply, the time has come for action to shed the patchwork of national and private interests and to arrive at a common interest. that starts with a decent price for pollution. oil giants like statoil and bp are now demanding the same. that starts with good access to everyone's markets. in short, it begins and can only begin with a europe and a european commission that takes the lead and has the courage to do so. to continue on the same path is to finance oligarchs and oil sheiks. let us choose the european approach and european investment to achieve a secure, sustainable and also cheap energy.
when i first came across the term smart borders package, i actually had to laugh. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer. our internal borders were abandoned in 1995, and the external borders we have today are as leaky as a basket thanks to the frontex shuttle service. the eu has no borders at all. smart borders in the eyes of the european union are primarily weak borders. millions of illegal immigrants are already roaming the european union and hundreds of thousands are ready to come this way. people who travel thousands of kilometres to enter the eu will not be stopped by a new electronic system. we'll need boats to send them back. my party, the freedom party, has no need for 791 million pounds of baked air. let's just re-establish our old boundaries. that would be really smart.
- a writer. i am glad that we can have this discussion with the commission today, and in a businesslike manner. i have no need to attack either the person of dg olaf or the committee on budgetary control. but the fact is that the supervisor has included a number of specific criticisms in his annual report and that olaf's replies do not allay the concerns of many colleagues and myself. for example, opening cases must be done carefully. you don't start an official investigation until there's a sufficiently serious suspicion of fraud. olaf points out that the new rules were not yet in force at that time. however, the supervisor does not refer to the new olaf regulation but to the case law of the court of justice. olaf admits that this case law was not followed, and that is serious. it is only an example, the real discussion between the supervisor and olaf is, in my view, mainly about how to monitor when you have no, or at least too little, insight into individual cases. of course, dg olaf is right to say that the privacy of suspects must be guaranteed, but olaf can still ensure that the information on selection criteria and on prioritisation is submitted to the supervisor with sufficient supporting evidence. the institutions have been wrangling over this for years now and i think that the european commissioner has a very clear task to perform. it seems that more specific guidance on the provision of information to the supervisor is needed to break the deadlock. finally, it is striking that olaf wants to keep a lot of cases under wraps. the proposal is to place a controller on procedural guarantees within olaf. but it certainly applies to the supervisor as well. it depends on the budget and the information from olaf. the european commissioner must take this into account. how can the supervisor become truly independent of the organization he is supervising? it is in everyone's interest that the relationship between the supervisor and olaf should improve rapidly. these organs are clearly not coming out on their own. i therefore hope that the commissioner will take up the matter quickly and that is why we are asking for a concrete action plan in the resolution. i trust that the commission will give us a commitment to come up with such a plan soon. we owe it to the citizens of europe.
in writing. ‒ unlike my ecr colleagues, i did support the saudargas report today. it has strong paragraphs about the transition to a low-carbon energy mix. that is the direction we must take and that is where we must also focus our innovation. at the same time, there is still room for fossil fuels in the transition period, so that energy security and stability are not compromised. i do not consider the use of shale gas as a safe option at this time because of the environmental risks. let us instead devote our energies to the further development of truly renewable sources of energy.
in writing. ‒ cu-sgp votes in favour of the joint resolution. the reason for this is that olaf must operate independently and carry out its tasks in accordance with the regulation. the monitoring of these by the monitoring committee may not be functioning sufficiently at present. as olaf is affiliated to the european commission, it is of great importance to ensure that olaf can carry out its work with the appropriate degree of independence from the eu institutions. however, the monitoring committee cannot establish this due to insufficient access to information. the findings of the monitoring committee, as presented in its annual report, are far from reassuring. there are even differences of opinion about the facts: is the length of the case due to the new calculation method or not? and did the assessment of the opening of cases proceed in accordance with the regulation or not? such a difference of opinion on the facts characterises the working relationship between olaf and the supervisory committee. the differences of opinion also relate to the interpretation of the regulation as regards the role of the monitoring committee. it is therefore appropriate to clarify that role in order to ensure effective independence of olaf.
let's look at one bright spot. who would have thought that so many people would be interested in a debate on burundi? and i like that. two weeks ago, we were ready to leave for burundi to observe the elections with our chief observer, david martin. it didn't look great, but it did look reasonable. there were some challenges, but -- and that turned around in a day or two -- the moment the president asked for his third term, the violence began to escalate. then the coup d'état happened, and now we're here. the idea that we could see parliamentary elections on 29 june seems to me to be an idea that we can just sweep off the table. we shall not be able to see that, because i expect a very violent election day. you can't legitimize that, and you can't prevent it with a little surveillance mission. so we're facing a huge crisis, with 100,000 refugees, with a youth militia putting crosses on the doors of people who would not be loyal to the regime, and the word ax-day. the question is what can we do there? but the question is also what we have not seen in the few years before this, that it would now so rapidly get out of hand. i feel sad. i think we should stay involved. but i don't think we're going to be watching the election.

in 1988, the netherlands became european champion in germany. i was four years old at the time, and i wouldn't really remember that at all. but it still seems like yesterday and i was there. that's the beauty of football. it brings joy to billions of people, not just in europe, but all over the world. it connects people, it makes people brothers. such a sport, football, deserves to be represented by a transparent, democratic and well-governed football association. the commissioner has indicated that fifa does not meet these criteria at all. so there is a lot to be done and the recent developments and the resignation of blatter also provide a unique opportunity to finally clean up the mess. several colleagues have already said: we cannot leave this to the americans and the swiss alone, europe must also show ambition. i think that both the council and the commission are still a little cautious on this point. i'd expect more from that. we should not have to think again about the role of the european union in this case. we must act and i have two specific proposals. commissioner, you yourself have said that fifa is no longer credible as the international representative of football. as a parliament we call tomorrow for the appointment of an interim president. would it not be wise for the european union to suspend its official contacts with fifa until reforms are made, until there is an interim president or a new director? the second proposal, which has already been mentioned by a number of colleagues: i am not saying that we should not be able to do this, but we should be able to do it.
mr president, the azerbaijani authorities are eager to host high-level, high-visibility international events. they bring status, visitors and media attention. but the limelight of the european games cannot and will not overshadow the very troubling human rights situation in azerbaijan, where an unprecedented number of people are imprisoned. opposition leaders, journalists, human rights defenders – and really anyone who is critical – risk intimidation and imprisonment, and some of the brightest minds have now fled the country. it is not appropriate to have eu officials participating in ceremonies starting in a couple of days, so long as political prisoners are not released. after all, the label ‘european’ should mean something. when rights are respected, we can build a much more powerful and meaningful partnership between the eu and azerbaijan. in the meantime we will not forget the political prisoners, and we urge the azerbaijani authorities to release them as soon as possible and to allow human rights ngos, such as amnesty international, access to the country.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on regional policy. the committee on regional policy strongly supports this new fund. we have already made available for this period 460 billion euro in the renewed regional funds, the so-called european structural and investment funds, with the co-financing of the member states. now, if you factor in a one-in-four multiplier, we have a real investment amount of about 1.5 trillion euros. with the addition of the efsi fund of some eur 315 billion for high risk capital, we have a wonderful package. europe is lacking in high-risk investments. our companies, our start-ups, the pilot projects in new technology, in smart industry have a real need for risk capital. the fund will fill this gap. let us now follow up the implementation with each other, together with the rapporteurs, to see whether we are actually achieving this synergy in terms of investment and jobs.

on behalf of the ppe group.– mr president, it is no secret of course that this report has taken quite a long time to be ready for plenary, and given the differences between political groups that is no surprise. for the epp, credible economic governance is first and foremost also implementing what you have agreed to do. in our view our economic governance framework is not the cause of the economic difficulties that europe finds itself in, it is an instrument for addressing imbalances within member states early on and making sure that we do not run up unsustainable debts. is this framework then perfect? surely not. procedures can be streamlined, as has been said by the commissioner; they can be better timed. we clearly need a more transparent democratic debate about the key points of our economic policy agenda and we need increased ownership and involvement by national parliaments. for the longer term everyone can see that our monetary union is not perfect or complete, but for steps to be taken to deepen our monetary union we first need to make sure that the current system actually works. in our view the economic governance framework needs to watch over – do i dare say it as a christian democrat? – the holy trinity of responsible public finances, structural reforms to improve competitiveness, and investments that can create growth and jobs – investments that are attracted by stability and competitiveness, not by running up debt. this will help make our economy stronger, making the eurozone as a whole stronger and more convergent. then, and only then, can we take the next step that we should take in deepening emu. it is a question of doing things in the right order, and this order i also see reflected in the five presidents’ report. i am glad that in the end we found some groups taking the responsibility, overcoming their differences and actually coming up with a report that gives us the element for the future agenda in the short and in the long term. and now let us get to work.
we are meeting today to discuss a very important issue, namely how the european parliament sees the future of the eurozone and the rules we have agreed to make it economically and fiscally sound again. unfortunately, the report on these agreements is a bit of a holes punch. it calls for more flexibility for the commission in applying budgetary agreements, when we have just seen how catastrophic loose enforcement of rules can be. the lesson should have been that rules should be applied rigorously and scrupulously to all countries, not that we should create more space for political cow-trading. instead of undermining the rules, parliament should instead stand up for a eurozone in which all countries stick to the agreements and all countries take measures to strengthen their economies. if countries do not, they will find themselves. that is why we believe that european money should only be available to countries that respect budgetary rules, that implement economic reforms and that actually implement the annual country-specific recommendations. that would really help the eurozone move forward. "blue card" is the answer. the rules must certainly be applied in all cases. the netherlands has also made use of the flexibility provided for in the stability and growth pact. after that, we followed the rules. so there is enough flexibility in the stability and growth pact and we see no reason to add more flexibility to it now. because again, there's already a very goodwill attitude towards countries, and countries are given time to make the adjustments at the right pace.
let me not begin with mrs berès' report, but with the report of the five presidents, because that report reads like an admission of guilt, for the good reader. the fact is that rules do not yet add up to policy, certainly not to policy which allows for economic stabilization and the fight against unemployment, nor to policy which is carried out democratically. instead of bringing member states together, the approach to the euro crisis has led to divergence between and within countries, and finally we are daring to admit that and there is movement. the present report by mrs berès and the others shows more than movement. it also gives direction and it also discusses the steps that need to be taken to strengthen the architecture of the euro area. the idea that fiscal policy is important for stabilisation and convergence, and therefore also for combating unemployment, is central. i would like to make one final comment. the countries have transferred sovereignty, but with the introduction of the euro, with a stronger democratic eurozone, sovereignty is being regained, not for one country in isolation but for all of us.
we are very pleased with the commission's action plan to tackle tax avoidance. the commission proposes a variety of instruments, and we believe that the most important instrument is transparency. transparency on rulings, transparency on where multinationals pay what taxes. we also believe that the role of tax authorities and governments is not the only one at stake, but that it is also the role of the multinationals themselves. when we have that transparency, then of course tax policy will also play a bigger role in the boardroom. the ethics in the boardroom are of the utmost importance. i think we are at the same level in the tax debate as we were in the boardrooms 15 years ago in the environmental debate. not everything that is legal is good. when there is more transparency, consumers can also choose with their feet, they can determine why they want to buy from a particular company or not. that is why i think we must use transparency in every possible way as an important weapon against tax evasion.
it is good that the european commission is serious about tackling tax evasion and this grievous injustice. only a common approach can prevent multinationals from playing off countries. a european basis for corporate taxation is therefore necessary. but the commission must realise that the action plan is welcome but still far from sufficient. let me confine myself to three points. firstly, the commission is doing half the work by omitting the consolidation of profits and losses. if we want to ensure that companies pay tax where they make a profit, we must not let this possibility slip away. secondly, the commission is doing half its job in tackling tax havens. yes, the blacklist of tax havens is welcome, but the real question is how we should deal with these tax havens. the netherlands, my country, is a transit port for untaxed profits. i would therefore strongly urge that a withholding tax on flows to these tax havens be made possible on the basis of european directives. thirdly, the european union must also support the forthcoming agreements in the oecd context by implementing them swiftly. the action plan does not say much about that. but it's important to close the gaps that are already there. finally, half a job does not mean half a problem. the practice of tax evasion teaches that every loophole will be searched. in short, there is still a lot of work to be done by this commission and by this parliament.
