mr president, when mr juncker first coined the term ‘capital markets union’, i had some doubts. was this a solution to the remaining obstacles to investments in smes in europe, or was it a way of reintroducing the old problems under a new flag? i think the answer very much depends on the way we approach the capital markets union. two key principles have to be at the heart of the capital markets union: it has to be safe, and it has to serve the real economy. enhancing the safety and the stability of the financial sector still requires a number of measures. on securitisation, the resolution strikes a balance – the right balance. the commission must show how shifting risks and freeing up capital leads to lending to smes. at the same time, high-quality securitisation requires an appropriate allocation of property rights and risks and requires powerful monitoring. there are problems with security-financing transactions which remain unaddressed: it is pro-cyclical in nature and it has the potential to increase systemic risk, and of course we still have to complete the banking union. we still do not have a europe-wide deposit guarantee. as regards serving the real economy, the potential of banking beyond banks is largely untapped in the eu, so there is a possibility for europe to diversify in financial instruments, and i think that is a good thing. it opens doors for smes and, with more risk-bearing capital, more smes can grow and develop in europe, contributing to the european economy as a whole. but it cannot be restricted to regulation in the financial sector. if we want to have more risk-bearing capital, we also have to look at the tax regime and at debt-equity buyers, and the commission has thus far been quiet on this subject. to conclude, the resolution rightly asserts that the capital markets union has to be fully compatible with past regulatory achievements. so yes, the capital markets union can offer an opportunity for a safer, service-oriented financial sector. this resolution offers a welcome starting point and strikes the right balance, a balance between the power of diversification and the necessity for proper regulation. this is the balance we have to protect throughout this project.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, let me start by thanking our rapporteur sirpa pietikäinen, and the two commissioners for their work and opening statements. as we are discussing the circular economy here, china is at the same time constructing artificial islands in the south china sea. for what reason? for one single reason: to secure resources. the single pillar of chinese foreign policy is to secure resources for its large population. and what about the eu? what are we doing in our external policy? not a lot. it is close to nothing, although we are the most vulnerable region in the world where resources are concerned. we provide only 30% of the resources that we use for ourselves, so in external policy, resource efficiency is absolutely absent. but of course – and this is the main focus of today’s debate – resource efficiency also has a very strong internal dimension: the efficient use of resources in europe itself. on this matter, i have to go back five years in time to 2010, when then commissioner potočnik presented his roadmap for a resource-efficient europe. i must say it was a brilliant document and a very strong analysis, with a roadmap on what europe should do to become resource efficient. there was a lot of legislation. it was not only about the environment but was maybe more about the economy. it talked about recycling, about better design, about creating economies of scale for the reuse of materials, the recovery of precious minerals like phosphates, and working on alternatives for our chemical industry through biomass. all that was in this brilliant document, and my question to the commission is: what will be the difference with the action plan that will be part of the circular economy package? is it just a delay of five years, or will it be a real improvement gained after five years? finally, let me thank the two vice-presidents for being here representing the commission. i hope that shows the importance of the topic in political terms. thank you, and i am very much looking forward to your proposal later this year.
we're here today to talk about an important subject. in the end, it is about the reuse of our raw materials, which will become scarce. we must think about how we will deal with our raw materials and our waste, because this is where the great opportunities and challenges lie for the coming years. we've come a long way in the recycling of our waste. the waste incineration plants are also recycling a lot, especially in the netherlands. incineration releases waste and valuable raw materials. it is suggested that incineration in particular is very unhealthy. but the technology used today is different and can also contribute to the circular economy, for example. the revenue model of reuse and recycling makes combustion in a good mix of very important. there is no point in phasing out too quickly if the rest of europe is not also enforcing the landfill ban properly. i want everyone to be able to come. in the netherlands, at least, garbage is not thrown in a pile. waste is separated before it is incinerated or recycled. a well-considered decision. billions have already been invested in waste management. if we change the frameworks, companies may be in trouble. and if valuable innovation is lost, we're going to fall behind. finally, another point. the exploitation of the circular economy between farmers is also essential for the agricultural sector. the recovery of scarce phosphate and other valuable raw materials from manure must be made possible in europe. so finally allow the use of mineral concentrate, include it in a fertiliser regulation and remove it from the waste legislation. only then can europe provide a credible picture of a truly circular economy for our farmers and our citizens.
the introduction of the milk quota in 1984 was a response to agricultural policy, which kept the price of milk artificially high. overproduction arose and the policy of buying-in and export subsidies became far too expensive. however, the policy did not dare to drastically adjust the price support and therefore switched to volume control. agricultural policy has become more market-oriented. the price of milk has been driven by supply and demand for the last seven years. from then on, brussels' control of volume was unnecessary. it is good that the milk quota has been abolished. dairy farmers on the other side of the world told me that the european milk quota was the best thing that had ever happened to them. the milk price is currently under pressure due to an unfortunate combination of circumstances: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the problem of the milk quotas. but to call it a milk crisis is an exaggeration. the 2013-2014 period was a record year for dairy farming and the long-term prospects for the dairy market are bright. i cannot support the call for a new system of volume control or an increase in intervention prices. we'll lose our connection to the world market. it makes us less competitive. in addition, direct payments will have to be reduced, which i cannot explain to other agricultural sectors. the common agricultural policy already has instruments to support farmers. we must not make it too complicated and give farmers the space to compete on the world market.
president. - thank you for your report. in the netherlands the price of milk is currently 30 cents a kilo. the real cost of the family farm is at least 40 cents a kilo of milk. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the milk market is not affected by the crisis. the sector is under pressure. last year i warned of the possible risks that the end of the milk quota could bring to our family and family businesses. as the dairy sector faces an uncertain future, we need to look at what instruments we can use if the current situation continues. this is also true of other sectors. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the milk sector is not undermined by the introduction of the single market. dairy farming plays an indispensable role in our countryside, food security, economy, tourism and agricultural nature management in the netherlands and in europe. this may cost money, but we must be careful not to take hasty measures that disrupt the market. (the president declared the common position adopted as amended) ) the european commission is finally opening up the possibility of closing the loop, of which the innovative mineral concentrate is an indispensable part. this is essential for the maintenance of the vital agricultural sector in the netherlands and in europe, especially for dairy farming, because the netherlands has the largest share of the cost of the slaughter of animals. commissioner, you will be able to make your move here at no cost.
draft report by the committee on transport and tourism. i would like to thank first of all the rapporteur, bernd lange, and also the shadow rapporteur for the epp, mrs quisthoudt, for their work. in the transport committee, isds was also a very big problem, but i hope that we can solve that with the new compromise amendment. the transport sector is very important in ttip. europe has great interests in the maritime sector, and i hope that the commissioner and her staff can break open the maritime sector in the usa. we're not trying to repeal the jones act. we're trying to amend it. take it down a notch. i also call attention to the buy american act - everyone knows that subject, i will not go into that - and to the safety procedures in the automobile industry. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to ask you a question. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, there is not a member in this house who did not contribute to this resolution on ttip, informed by the daily discussions with citizens, civil society organisations, smes and companies. their many wishes, but also concerns, are reflected in this intensive stocktaking. is it a perfect text for our group? no, but it is important that this house sends a strong message to the negotiators, and cooperation requires some compromise. i would like to remind our socialist and democrat friends of that as well. the text we have agreed maps out what we believe a good ttip should look like. i am sure it is not the last debate we will have on ttip and the state of play of the negotiations, which will probably last years more. we call on you, commissioner, to get clear results from the us side, as results are the best way to demystify and clarify, while not, of course, replacing transparency initiatives, which we also encourage you to take more of. concretely, we need market access for eu companies in the us, government procurement at all levels, and an end to protectionist measures that seriously restrict the space for our businesses, notably in the transport sector. we need benefits for smes so they can more easily expand business across the atlantic. most growth in the global economy is foreseen as coming outside of europe. we are happy to see developing economies succeed, but we must avoid a race to the bottom and do not believe in development and growth at any cost without respect for people and the planet. our environmental, food safety, labour and other standards are cherished and cannot be lowered. we will also remind you of your commitments on public services. it is key that europe lead in strengthening rules-based trade globally. we need progress in finding a solution to the outdated isds mechanism which, frankly, has no fans left. i support your plans for an international court and hope the member states and other countries, including the us, can be convinced that this is the way forward. a lot of work remains to be done on the road to a good result from the negotiations. i hope we can all intensify our outreach and discussions to engage with stakeholders so that we have a well-informed and interactive debate.
madam president, i should like to address my fellow meps and remind them of whom they represent. it is not business europe or the american chamber of commerce, whose agenda is the basis for this agreement. it is not the lawyers, who are benefiting from an exploding number of isds cases. nor is it the companies, which will be writing and rewriting our democratic laws. instead, you represent the 2.3 million people who have signed the european citizens’ initiative to stop ttip. it is the 150 000 people who have contributed to the public consultation on isds, and who were 97% negative on the whole idea. it is the hundreds and thousands of people who are now writing letters and emails to you to stop ttip and isds. it is the people you were talking to when you were canvassing. it is the people who voted for you. you are a democratically-elected representative. vote no to ttip and no to isds.
ttip is more than a trade agreement. it is the creation of a single market between the eu and the us. the single market poses serious risks to workers and to our social system. within the eu we are still struggling with the social problems. we must therefore be vigilant to a new market adventure with a superpower like the united states. a negotiated outcome can count on our support if basic social rights are also recognised in the us, if it is clear that ttip will create jobs for us and what is being done for the workers affected, if our european standards on consumer protection and the environment are not undermined, if public services such as education and health care are excluded, and if there are no private isds tribunals for investors to sue countries. we need to be clear: no isds in the ttip. investors can rely on national public law just like anyone else. the international business community does not need its own legal system outside the democratic rule of law. we are also preventing claims from influencing states and the rules we establish democratically. only a resolution that says very clearly: "we don't need isds and we don't want it", can count on my support.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, as the commissioner may probably note with a smile on his face, parliament’s resolution opens by saying that we want to focus on the big themes – and then we need about 29 pages to explain what the big themes are. i very much welcome the aim of the commission, supported by the majority in this house, to cut red tape. i think that is always a good idea anywhere, but it should not become a goal in itself. quite frankly, cutting red tape – important as it may be – is not the answer to all the big challenges of today. it is not the answer to the refugee crisis. it is not the answer to the crisis in the eurozone. it is not the answer to the dismantling of the schengen area. it is not the answer to mr putin. so we need to cut red tape, but we need more than that. i think what we need to keep in mind is that what we are doing here today is creating the european union that future generations will be living in. it is not just about us and our little differences, or indeed the things that bind us, it is about future generations. i will make a first remark about better law-making. as far as i am concerned, that is much more about the process than it is about the outcome, because the outcome is a matter of political choice. in the process i think there is a lot that needs to be improved, and the commission can do a lot there too, for example by introducing a true culture of transparency and access to documents because, despite all the nice words, i find that the three institutions – and that includes the european parliament – still have a very conservative reflex when it comes to opening up and being transparent. a second point – it is a minor one and is not in the resolution, but i think i am speaking on behalf of all of us – would be getting true and serious responses to our written parliamentary questions, rather than the non-answers that we usually get. it also means responding to legislative proposals by the european parliament. i think that the commissioner himself has said that he intends to do this, because there is something odd in the fact that a citizens’ initiative requiring a million signatures obliges the commission to respond, but this parliament, elected by no less than 170 million voters who went to the ballot boxes last year, can be ignored. finally, i think the commission can – and should – do a lot better on review and evaluation. on the big themes, we are talking about improving the eurozone – and that is not just greece, that is eurozone governance – and we need to go much further than the five presidents’ proposals. the commission should stand shoulder to shoulder with the european parliament in avoiding the dismantling of the schengen area and free movement. finally, this house has called for a mechanism for the enforcement of article 2 fundamental rights and the rule of law. this house will elaborate proposals, and we hope that the commission will do the same.
on behalf of the verts/ale group.– mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his introduction. i think all of us in this room can agree on one point, namely that europe should indeed be big on big things and small on small things. but the debate, of course, is about what we call big and what we call small. that is a political debate and a political decision. let us not hide between impact assessments, scrutiny boards or whatever. this should be an open political debate on what we call big and what we call small and where we will put europe at the forefront. there is a lot of criticism about how europe works at the moment, but is that because there are many rules coming out of brussels or is it because of the situation in greece? is it because of the rising unemployment in europe? is it because of the rising inequalities in europe? i think it is the latter. that is the big thing for me. europe should become a europe that is addressing inequalities and moving towards a social europe and a sustainable europe. that is the core, the big thing and the big challenge for europe. i think we should always be united there. mr timmermans said that we should get rid of the myth that this commission is not being progressive and not being ambitious on legislation. well, i still have to say that we need to see some proof of that. one of the biggest moments when we are going to read that out is, of course, when you come forward with the so-called more ambitious proposal on the circular economy package. we will certainly watch closely to see whether that is indeed more ambitious. mr timmermans, you said in our group, ‘let me prove you wrong’, because you know we are sceptical. well, i am happy to be proved wrong, but we have to see it. the same goes for issues like migration, and also the commission’s first reallocation plans. these are good and we support them, but we all know that this is just the start of a big debate. the commission should also come forward here, for example by creating legal routes for labour migration, which is a very important part. the same on taxation. the taxation package until now has not been very ambitious. we should have far more on transparency and country-by-country reporting. it is the same when we are discussing emu, which needs to be more democratic and more social. we will challenge all these issues and have to look at them. in the end this needs to be done together, so let us indeed look forward to collaborating on the interinstitutional agreement – but let us do it on an equal footing. let us not hide behind bureaucratic impact assessments, and let us make it a political debate where all three institutions are at the same level.
luxleaks has shown what transparency can do. the once secret arrangements of the luxembourg tax authorities have unleashed a storm of indignation and brought the fight against tax evasion closer. the principle is that companies pay tax where they make profits. but if there is no information about it, then that principle is loose and empty. transparency and country-by-country reporting are therefore crucial to enforce that principle. if the principle is that wages should be fair - and i add that the top of the business community should set the example - then that principle is hollow and empty if there is no information about it. it is therefore important that the ratio between the top and the average employees is reported. i think transparency is still a work in progress. but i would like to thank the rapporteurs and the shadow rapporteurs for the steps that have been taken. transparency is a powerful weapon and the european parliament has shown that.

for the greens/efa group a study also cited in mrs schlein's report shows that tax rules in the netherlands - my country of origin - cost developing countries between 150 million and 550 million euros a year. we can be proud to be a major player in development aid in this world, but every penny that leaves africa because taxes are not paid where the work is done is actually a sin and we cannot make up for it by giving development aid. i was rapporteur for the report on anti-money laundering, new european legislation. we have called for and arranged for the establishment of a public register in which we can see who really owns a company, so that we can make sure that multinationals do not evade or avoid taxes. because society will have to put an end to that. we can talk a long time in addis ababa, but if we continue to allow multinationals to shop around looking for the place where they pay the least tax, then we can never get our development money to match the health, infrastructure and education in sub-saharan africa. so i ask the commissioner: fighting for good tax laws there.

i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr kukan, for a resolution which contains many valid points, including the much needed differentiation, the need for a more political and less technocratic approach to european neighbourhood policy and the centralization of european values of democracy, the rule of law and human rights, without double standards. the most important thing is that this resolution reaffirms that eu policy must benefit the people of our neighbouring countries and not the political elite there. nor must it be motivated solely by self-interest, such as the deterrence of refugees. we demand reforms of the rule of law and guarantees of human rights for the simple reason that the people are better off in a just and well-governed country. for the countries with which we have signed association agreements, ukraine, georgia and moldova, this is even an obligation. these agreements go beyond trade. they also have an important political component. some politicians argue that the neighbourhood policy should be used only as a geopolitical instrument. they want to put a hand on the head of wrong regimes and look the other way when fundamental rights are violated. they are not so strict in demanding agreed reforms. the geopolitical goal of stability in a troubled region, fighting terrorism or reducing russian influence, in this view, sanctifies the means, namely turning a blind eye to abuses such as selective justice, nepotism or human rights violations. a credible neighbourhood policy requires a political vision that is not based solely on geopolitics and spheres of influence. the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force. in these difficult times for the country, it is important that the european union stands ready to help. i am convinced that this is not necessarily best done with huge sums of money, with muscle-balling language towards russia or with unrealistic promises of eu or nato membership. the best thing europe can do is to keep the government in kiev in line, to ensure that the agreements made are respected, and to finally get through the reforms that the country so badly needs and that the ukrainian people are also longing for. the new policy must therefore be ambitious. what i want to convey to commissioner hahn tonight is this: the common values on which the european neighbourhood policy is built, namely respect for human rights and the rule of law, must not be undermined by geopolitical interests.

in writing. ‒ ttip is an important agreement for the development of transatlantic trade relations and that is why i support the negotiations. but they are far from complete. a fair assessment can only be made on the basis of the facts and the result of the negotiations. this resolution sends an important signal to the european commission about what the european parliament considers important and about what concerns exist. there are clear requirements. we do not want to see standards lowered in areas such as the environment or food safety, no hormone-based meat on the european market, no chlorinated chickens and no genetically modified products. the commissioner malmström has repeatedly assured the ep of this. european legislation to protect citizens and the environment will not be sacrificed for free trade. the harmonization of standards and norms will only take place when they are already very close together. investment is important to our economy. providing a good investment climate, clear and reliable regulation and fair investment protection is important. the reform plans proposed by commissioner malmström provide a good starting point for reforming the isds system and addressing existing weaknesses. the right of member states to develop legislation is clearly laid down in it. these proposals should be reflected in the final outcome of the negotiations.

rapporteur.– mr president, i would like to say to the shadow rapporteurs in particular that i enjoyed our discussions and cooperation on this report on human rights and technology, so thank you very much. in addition, i would like to thank the people from all over the world who have shared their input and suggestions. many did so online and they have made the draft text that we voted on in the committee on foreign affairs, and will vote on tomorrow in this house, a lot stronger. i think crowdsourcing works. technological developments have a major impact on human rights, both to advance them and to restrict them. they can advance access to information, freedom of expression and association, but the documentation and sharing of human rights abuses or uncovering of corruption have benefited too from mobile phones and access to the open internet for more people. disaster relief, aid efforts, election monitoring, they increasingly rely on technologies to help emancipate and empower people. however, technological systems can be and are also misused as tools for human rights violations through censorship, mass surveillance, tracking and tracing of individuals, unauthorised access to devices, jamming, interception and a lot more. so besides technological specifications, the context in which icts are used determines to a great extent whether these tools advance or violate human rights: what country, which user or buyer, and what legal safeguards or democratic oversight are in place. these contextual aspects should be assessed carefully in eu policies. more transparency and accountability in the market of the most intrusive and damaging technologies is key, both for security and for human rights. this is too often presented as a false dichotomy between security and freedom or actually justifying restrictions to peoples’ rights and freedoms in the name of advancing security when in fact they should reinforce each other and, at the very least, eu-made systems should not be sold in an unrestricted market. without smart policies, proliferation of these systems is inevitable and in fact i am sure that we here in the eu institutions, the european external action service, struggle to protect our own people, our own diplomats and our own information from the very same systems that we are discussing here. intelligence services, criminal networks, hackers with bad intentions, all can buy and use intrusive technologies that are made in europe. the credibility of europe’s foreign policy is undermined directly when an italian company unjustly gets a licence to sell to sudan or russia, or when a french company sells digital keys to open any door, even the ones we may think are protected or locked by passwords, or much stronger measures. but, much worse, human rights defenders in mexico, azerbaijan, colombia, egypt, turkey, iran or russia should be protected by eu policies and not harmed with the help of eu-made technologies. in concluding, eu policy should mainstream human rights in the way that technologies are designed and used and mainstream the role of technologies throughout all human rights policies, and this report presents many concrete ideas. one of the strong recommendations is that there should not be a ban on encryption technologies or requirements to hand over the keys. instead, we, through our eu policy, should train and equip journalists, human rights defenders and others facing repression and violations with the knowledge and the tools to protect themselves and their communications. europe has the opportunity and should have the ambition to be a global leader in ensuring technologies benefit human rights and this is only possible if we lead by example.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group the birth of dolly the sheep, the world's first cloned animal, in 1997 caused a great wave of reaction around the world. it led to horror, disbelief and outrage. and that makes it a very special subject, because it is one of the few subjects on which both the european parliament and the european people are in complete agreement. it is morally reprehensible and should be banned. european citizens do not want it, consumers do not want it. the farmers don't need it, and the animals are paying the price. that's the gist of what cloning is all about. first of all, the consumers. they reject it en masse and want a free choice to reject it. so if it's going to be on the market, they want a good label. it will lead to a lot of problems in the agricultural sector, much more than we have now, because it will lead to the demise of small farmers. in addition, there is immense animal suffering. because the only purpose is to create offspring of highly productive animals. even after three decades of research, there's been no progress in terms of animal welfare, in terms of effectiveness, in terms of efficiency. still, the vast majority of animals die at birth and only a small percentage survive the first year of life, suffering from disease and abnormalities. many surrogate mothers suffer unbearably, they suffer from the procedure itself, and they all have to undergo a cesarean section. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. the party for the animals calls for a total ban without any exception. a ban on cloning animals and a ban on imports. i also think that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
"blue card" question. what is it? my colleague is advocating a temporary ban and not a permanent ban, but we heard very clearly at the hearing that there have been no real improvements in efficiency in the last few decades, that the technology has made little progress and that it is still as bad as it was 30 years ago. do you not think that it would be much better to maintain a permanent ban and you also pointed out that there are ethical concerns, but these ethical concerns are present throughout the european union. would not a special procedure, as outlined by the commissioner, be legitimate in this case?
farmers all over europe are having a hard time at the moment, but the market orientation of agricultural policy is something we have been working towards for decades and a great asset. i support the european commission in this. this is the future. but farmers must be able to operate on a level playing field. same monks, same caps. i know. we'll also need to lower the control pressure. agricultural policy is too complicated and hinders farmers from being entrepreneurs. a good example is the rigid greening requirements. we must remain competitive in the global market. this should be the aim of european policy-makers, but they too often live in a paper reality. the european parliament's proposal to trace the offspring of cloned animals and their products is just such an unworkable proposal. it is absolutely impracticable to ban the products of the offspring of cloned animals, such as milk and meat. the products of cloned animals are indistinguishable from non-cloned animals. the cloning of animals is banned in the european union and rightly so. until it is conclusively proven that cloning has no adverse effect on the health and welfare of the animal, i think that should remain the case. the european commission has made a balanced proposal based on scientific advice from the european food safety authority. the proposal is based on facts. the european parliament's proposal, on the other hand, is based on fear mongering. i shall therefore vote against the european parliament's proposal.
in parliament we think about the future. i am glad that this is so, because it is very necessary, especially when we are talking about cloning animals. but in the end, it's about the people. but now we must get the facts straight. only then can we make a decision that takes account of the real situation. and that situation is that there are a lot of products from cloned animals on the european market at the moment and have been for a long time. which brings me to the traceability of cloned animals. we are for a europe with fewer rules. are we now going to advocate that cloned animals should be traceable to the third generation? i wonder if this is feasible and i am sure it is not. look at all the milk from all the farmers that ends up in one tank. even in the case of non-cloned animals, it is already difficult to guarantee data on breed and quality. if that is not possible now, why do we want to start with cloned animals? and i have not even mentioned products from cloned animals from third countries. in short, let's keep this discussion reasonable. only then can we make policies that are really oriented towards the future.
i am in favour of a total ban on cloning and we therefore support this report. my party has ethical objections to cloning, as it goes against the order of creation. it is also associated with great suffering and a high mortality rate among cloned animals. a tracing system must be introduced as soon as possible to prevent clones from third countries from entering the community. i strongly urge the eu to speak out in international forums for a worldwide ban on cloning. third countries must also stop, first of all for ethical reasons, and secondly, there must be unanimity to ensure a level playing field.
in writing. ‒ seal fur is an unnecessary fashion product for which seals are brutally killed. that is why the sale and import of seal products was banned in europe years ago. but there were two exceptions, the hunting of seals in the baltic sea and the hunting by the indigenous peoples (the inuit). the world trade organization has spoken out on this, finding for the first time in history that animal welfare is a legitimate reason to restrict trade. the party for the animals believes that there is no excuse or justification for the hunting of seals. that is why i have proposed that these two exceptions be deleted. the hunting of european seals is completely unnecessary. the second. the inuit hunt in particular does not meet european standards for animal welfare. if the inuit need seals for their survival, then we should not encourage them to sell seal fur to europe. the european parliament did not have a majority in favour of abolishing the inuit exception. nevertheless, i have agreed to the final proposal because it further restricts large-scale commercial seal hunting.
in writing. ‒ 'blue growth', or economic growth by using our oceans and seas. this is a further exploitation of natural resources at sea. however, the limits of the earth do not allow for further exploitation, and this is certainly true of the eu's already heavily polluted and overfished waters. the european fishing fleet is already twice the size of the fish stocks it can support. the ecosystems in our seas are being disrupted by pollution. our economic activities at sea should not grow, but shrink. this requires a different way of thinking about economics. we must abandon the naive belief in growth and take as a starting point the sustainability of the earth and the well-being of all its inhabitants. that is why i voted against this report.

- i'm the reporter. welcome, commissioner, and good of you to be here today. i present my report on the draft council decision on the conclusion of a protocol between the european union and the republic of cape verde setting out the fishing opportunities and financial contribution; i have tabled a non-legislative resolution alongside a draft legislative resolution. and that is precisely why parliament can say more than just yes or no to the fisheries agreement. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs, the european commission and certainly the secretariat of our committee on fisheries for the cooperation. unfortunately, cape verde is not a highly developed country. it ranks 131st on the un development index, which totals 187 countries. the economy is growing, especially tourism. it is regrettable that just last week the archipelago was hit by a major hurricane for the first time in over a hundred years. fortunately, the damage from this hurricane was not that great. but what you do see is increased uncertainty. are storms like this going to happen more often? could that be related to climate change? as regards the draft legislative resolution, i recommend that parliament approve the new protocol. i think that is beneficial both for the european union and for our fishermen who use it. but it is also important for cape verde to get economic returns from the fish stocks in their waters. fortunately, mr ponga, on behalf of the committee on development and cooperation, and mr fernandes, on behalf of the committee on budgets, also came to this conclusion. i thank both rapporteurs for their opinions. the european union concluded a fisheries partnership agreement with cape verde in 1990 and six protocols have been implemented since then. so we're dealing with the seventh protocol, which has a four-year term. the total amount involved in the agreement is eur 3.3 million and can be roughly divided into eur 1 million for compensation for access to the waters, eur 1 million for sectoral support and eur 1.2 million for compensation by the shipowners. compared with the previous protocol, the amount of sectoral aid in particular has increased considerably. it has actually doubled, and that means more projects to improve fisheries monitoring and also to combat illegal fishing. in addition, there are projects to improve safety, particularly for local fishermen, and to train and educate these people, and i am also pleased to report that european ship owners are also contributing more to the quotas they use. then the sharks. that is a subject on which i have tabled a non-legislative resolution. because the shark catch has increased greatly in these waters off cape verde, doubling in fact. the commission has not yet received any comments from interested parties. this worrying development has led to the inclusion of three measures in this protocol. the first. the number of longliners is being reduced from 35 to 30, and it is the fishermen and the fishing methods that are bringing out many sharks. the second. the shark catch is monitored and thresholds of 1500 and 2000 tonnes are built in, after which control measures can be taken. the third. there's going to be an investigation into the life of the sharks in that area over there. of course, i know that this is a sensitive subject, and these measures in the protocol are a first step, but i think the thresholds that are built into these measures are quite low. they'll be here soon. i think it is important for this parliament to keep our finger on the pulse by following developments closely and i ask the commission to do the same. there is a real need to take action, because sharks are a vulnerable fish stock and should not be overfished. they are part of god's creation and vital to the ecosystem. i therefore ask you to support my amendment to tighten up the text.

- i'm the reporter. first of all, i would like to thank my fellow shadow rapporteurs and all those involved in this midterm review for their cooperation. we have had a lengthy process with 633 amendments and it was not easy to get the shadow rapporteurs to agree on a coherent story. but we did it. i would also like to thank the staff of the various political groups, in particular the epp group and parliament, for their cooperation. the first comments on the white paper were a bit reticent. not much new policy and not much sharp choices. but it is a midterm review and so it is also intended to mark continuity in policy. i think that went well. a european union which would present a completely new transport policy every four years is not credible. it's a midterm review and it's about implementation and continuity. the transport sector is very important for europe. in the netherlands we always say: without transport, everything is at a standstill. the transport sector has a high earning capacity and is of great importance for the development of the european economy; the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. at the same time, the transport sector must become more sustainable. the transport sector accounts for 24% of pollution in europe, the energy sector for 29%. well, then you understand our position. sustainability is not only a threat, but also an opportunity for innovation and innovation in the transport sector, and that is what we will have to work on for the next 10 to 20 years. the commission has already made a number of proposals. unfortunately, we have to say that the modal shift, the shift from road and air transport to rail and inland waterways, has not yet been achieved. that is a very unfortunate observation, because we want cleaner transport in inland waterways and on trains. the european union's investment in the coming years will therefore have to give priority to railways and inland waterways, whether or not combined with maritime transport. that is a conclusion we all share. then the intelligent transport systems. we want more innovation and we also want an electronic bill of lading by 2020; it's a big challenge for us to make things happen. there are a number of proposals, for example for doubling the number of urban transport services, which have raised a lot of dust, but my colleagues will speak about them later.
i would like to thank the rapporteur and my colleague, mr wim van de camp, for their excellent cooperation. we had said beforehand that we did not want a document which was grey and which was really a compromise but which therefore said nothing. we want a compromise which also makes some points clear. and i think we have succeeded in doing that together and we certainly have mr van de camp to thank for that. it has not become a green text on all points, but it cannot be until we have a majority. but i do think that there are a number of points which are extremely important and it is also good for the commission and the commissioner to see that the report is embraced. however, there are some very clear points which need to be improved. because if we look at the current state of transport - and mr van de camp has already said this - we are actually quite behind on this modal shift, the transition to the use of inland waterways and railways. that's not good. and if you look at reducing the environmental pollution from the transportation sector. that's not going well either. it is still unclear how europe will work towards this in the longer term after 2020. there are still challenges here too, and we expect more from the commission. it is also very important that this should be added to the social policy. we all know that the opening up of the services market has a social dimension. this too is being given more attention and the commission is clearly invited to come forward with proposals. these have also been announced, but they are still lacking in substance. i think it is important that this report is clearly seen as a call to the commission to not just embrace the developments so far. it is also important to come up with new proposals so that we can say at the end of the white paper: yes, the transport sector is really on the right track.

in writing. ‒ vvd abstained on the resolution as a whole for two reasons: firstly: the proposal that asylum seekers, refugees and labour migrants are entitled to work during the procedures and granting of a definitive status. vvd is of the opinion that only if and when the asylum seeker, refugee or labour migrant has been granted a final and irrevocable status, may that person enter the labour market. otherwise, expectations will be raised that the person concerned would be entitled to stay anyway. secondly: the fact that the resolution proposes taking into account the preferences and consent of refugees for the countries they will be allocated to. this would undermine the allocation and quota system, as most refugees prefer in the end to stay in germany or other countries such as austria, the netherlands, belgium, denmark and sweden. vvd did not vote against the resolution because it is of the opinion that a new, integral system for asylum, refugee policy and labour migration, including protection of the eu’s external borders through frontex and settlement in the region, must be created. those persons not granted a status to come to or stay in the eu must be expelled.

we did it. nevertheless, we believe that some exceptions are justified. as co-legislators, i think we should be aware of the practical implications, especially if the european union wants to encourage the use of biomass. the requirements for, for example, biomass combustion plants have therefore been adapted. we have also managed to avoid overlapping with other regulations, thus avoiding duplication of legislation with, for example, the directive on industrial emissions. there are other loopholes in the law. for example, an installation designed for emergency situations can really only be used during an emergency situation and not operate under normal conditions. we have managed to reduce the administrative burden and bureaucracy. rules should be applied unambiguously by a unambiguous licensing system. finally, it is good that member states want to learn from each other. in some member states, the business sector is already much more advanced. it is up to other member states to show that ambition too. i am pleased with the outcome of the negotiations. we are keeping room for innovation and across europe, work is being done on air quality by tackling the problem at its source.

author. the conflict in the central african republic is one of the biggest humanitarian crises right now, along with syria and sudan, but it gets a lot less attention in the central african republic. it seems forgotten. the rebels in the central african republic are responsible for executions, rapes, kidnappings and mass looting. 2.7 million people, almost half the population, are in need of aid, but aid agencies, such as the red cross, cannot even provide their services in the capital bangui. patients have to walk to the hospital. yesterday, a blue helmets officer was killed in a gun battle with rebels. these rebel armies pay their people with the trade in diamonds and hardwood. there is a temporary halt to the diamond trade, but the ban is being lifted and stocks are building. as soon as diamonds can be traded officially again, these diamonds will be on the market. the people will not benefit from that. in fact, it's funding rebels again. european companies are also buying this hardwood and these diamonds from these rebels. amnesty international has just done a major study on this. there are belgian companies doing business there. i call on european companies to stop this now, because the money has to go to the rebels or the conflict will never end.
author.– madam president, this debate on this resolution is a result of our commitment to, and our friendship with, the thai people. because of this friendship and commitment there can be only one priority for the eu with regard to thailand and that is for thailand to return as soon as possible to a democratic society with an elected civil and representative government. we therefore urge the thai authorities not only to stick to the presented timeline for a new constitution, with elections in mid-2017, but also to present a constitution that is based on democracy, equality, freedom and fundamental rights. this is something we are concerned about because, if a constitution is to be future-proof, the process needs to be inclusive. it needs to be transparent, and people need to have the opportunity to participate. we have seen increasingly worrying reports of deteriorating human rights in thailand. we call on the thai authorities to lift all measures that restrict freedom of assembly and freedom of speech, and to allow for the genuine political involvement of all citizens.

on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, i understand that the future of the european union will be decided in a dance contest and i cannot wait to see mr juncker taking mr cameron in his arms, but i would just like to underline that the future of europe is decided in an open, transparent and public debate in a democratic process and not in backroom deals. the council draft conclusions open with a very encouraging sentence saying that tackling the migration and refugee crisis is a common obligation which requires a comprehensive strategy and a determined effort over time in a spirit of solidarity and responsibility. and i thought, yes, this is a radical change of strategy in the council: ‘wir schaffen das’. unfortunately, the determination of the council ends after the first sentence. the decision on the 160 000 was already inadequate and everybody knew it, and many more people will come our way. but the member states have not even properly delivered on their previous pledges, never mind for additional refugees, and the only response of the council seems to be: let’s keep our eyes closed and maybe the problem will go away. but citizens are concerned, not just members of parliament. i see the ‘remain’ or ‘leave’ campaign has already started in this room. but citizens – i can see that the presidency of the council is on the phone to a citizen right now – are concerned. they see the images of large numbers of refugees coming this way. they also see refugee centres being established in their neighbourhoods, and they are anxious. but the majority of european citizens, i am convinced, want to welcome refugees and they want europe to show solidarity. but they also want to know that europe is able to cope with the situation. they want to see leadership, but instead the government leaders are dithering and hesitating and ducking the issue and coming up with half-hearted and inadequate solutions. how can you expect citizens to be confident that europe can handle the situation if government leaders themselves do not show that they are up to the job? the commission and parliament have put forward proposals repeatedly for a comprehensive strategy, but the council is silent. but we urgently need a comprehensive policy here that includes legal avenues for migration, a real eu asylum policy with a permanent distribution key and a real eu border control system. on the talks with turkey, i think that is very welcome and it is essential. but i wonder: the commission, and in particular the council, are making promises about financial support without actually specifying where the money will come from. earlier promises of funds, as mr juncker just set out, have not been delivered upon, so how can you promise more money to a country if you have not even delivered on your earlier promises? it is very clear that the current financial framework does not allow for these kinds of exercises. and besides, i would like to echo the words of other speakers who ask themselves: how can we be sure that the human rights of refugees will be guaranteed in turkey? the council draft conclusions also mention the aim of developing safe and sustainable reception capacities in the affected regions and providing lasting prospects for refugees. i was a little mystified by that, because i wonder exactly what ‘lasting prospects’ means when you are in a refugee camp. i hope for your sake that refugees have the same notion of ‘lasting prospects’. reception in the region and cooperation with third countries mainly means outsourcing our problems, and i think it is a bit bitter that we applaud the nobel peace prize laureate, tunisia, and at the same time expect them to solve our problems and receive the people we turn away. finally, the draft conclusions do not remotely constitute an answer to the current situation, so the alde group expects you to go back, do your homework and come up with a realistic and comprehensive strategy, so that we can truly say to european citizens: ‘wir schaffen das’. on behalf of the alde group.– madam president, i think we all know the immense importance of the climate summit in paris – even the ecr, mr duncan – and it is absolutely crucial that we reach a very ambitious, legally-binding, comprehensive agreement there, an agreement that goes beyond the indcs that are being collected now – because we all know that, if we add them up, we will never stay below the 2 degrees centigrade, as the commissioner has already said. one of the crucial elements, again, will be international climate finance, and it will maybe be the sole case that can make or break the agreement in paris. europe could be the broker of such a deal, but unfortunately, in the council mandate for paris, the climate commitments after 2020 are missing. apparently there was no agreement yet, when the climate ministers came together in september, among the ministers of finance for this very crucial finance. in our own parliament’s resolution, we do mention it. we set aside a number of allowances from the emissions trading scheme (ets) to create a fund, an eu international climate action reserve that will raise revenues in a fair way and will create a fund independent of national governments. i think that is a crucial element of our resolution. as mentioned before, in november the ecofin council will come together, and i urge our ministers of finance to seriously look into the suggestion made in this parliament’s resolution. just like the others, i would like to urge the commissioner to be present there and to make sure that ecofin copies that suggestion.

in writing.– to maintain a liveable earth we have to halt climate change now. as one of the main culprits of climate change, europe carries a great responsibility in achieving global binding agreements on the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. regrettably, the report ‘towards a new international climate agreement’ only contains one single paragraph on agriculture, whose impact on climate change cannot be overstated enough. especially, the negative impact of meat production on climate change persists, and prospects for the future are grim. the world population is estimated to reach 9.6 billion by 2050. diets are predicted to become more affluent, containing more meat and dairy products. this shift towards unsustainable diets will entail a tremendous, if not impossible burden on the planet’s resources. there is a clear link between livestock farming and global warming. meat and dairy production are responsible for at least 14% of all our greenhouse gas emissions. it would be negligent to not tackle this issue when fighting climate change – especially when we realise that people and animals in the poorer areas of the world will be affected first and the worst, owing to extreme weather conditions due to climate change.

rapporteur.– mr president, the debate today on the tools and services that can be used for capital punishment reminds us once again that trade policy is an integral part of the eu’s foreign policy. it is vital that we continue to develop values-based trade policies, as is very much the theme of the new trade policy of the commission. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for the collegial cooperation during which we focussed on updates needed to close the loopholes in the current eu regime. the anti–torture regulation combines prohibitions of goods on the one hand with licensing requirements for trade in listed goods on the other hand. it governs a ban on trade in so–called single-use torture and execution items and related technical assistance under annex 2. but it also looks at an authorisation system for multi—purpose items that could be misused for torture and executions: for example, lethal injections. we see this in annex 3. the overhaul of the existing eu controls of trade in torture instruments has been long overdue. in this update we must ensure a correct mix of eu legislative, administrative, judicial and external measures. the absence of a multilateral export controls regime does complicate this task. still, we seek coherence of the eu’s export controls, including military, dual use, firearms and torture goods lists, without subjecting items to duplicate controls and making things more complicated. the commission proposes to strengthen the controls of torture goods to include certain medical substances and to broaden the scope to include brokering. i support this kind of approach of necessary and proportionate controls without creating unnecessary burdens for legitimate trade. in this regard it is also key that we seek a more level playing field in europe. at the same time as we act now, our policies and systems should be future–proof and flexible, so as to be able to adjust to changing technologies or changing realities elsewhere in the world. specific item lists can provide clarity for exporters and importers to make it easier for them to comply. we have also made sure that it is very clear and that there is no doubt about legitimate access to medicines and pharmaceuticals which, of course, for the eu is an important market, and it must not be hindered by measures to curb the abuse of lethal injection. while we observe that foreign trade transactions are becoming more and more complex, we have to look for an effective set of enforcement measures, and this should also include the marketing and financial services that do take place and are related to this industry. we have asked for reporting and exchange of information between member states via a secure encrypted system, so that rejected licences and other notifications or important information can be shared and can help states coordinate. as the commission can more flexibly adapt the regulations and the items that are at stake, we do ask, as a parliament, to be duly informed and involved. finally, we look to industry to take its own responsibility to make sure that the eu does not trade, broker, transfer or otherwise get involved with goods or services that are used for the death penalty or torture. i believe – and i think all of our colleagues believe – that in this text we put forward concrete proposals for improvement in this file, all with the goal to make sure that the eu acts as a values–based global player and that this specific, quite technical, aspect of trade is upgraded and implemented in a way that is both flexible and effective.
rapporteur.– mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner and my colleagues. on a slightly lighter note, i thought it was a pleasure – and a bit of a relief – to hear that we can actually talk about trade and agree from one side of the house to the other side of the house, and even see that trade policies can advance values directly, so i note that with a smile. commissioner, thank you for your very detailed reply to the text that we have worked on together in this house. i note many points of overlap – if not literally, then at least in spirit – and that is a reason for optimism. i think that it has been well established by everyone who spoke that we share the goal of ending the death penalty and torture, and this is one way to contribute to that. it is not as widely shared a goal in this house. unfortunately, it is not entirely unanimously shared, but at least by a large majority. on the implementation: yes, of course measures must be feasible and even agile. that is our goal too, but i think we are being both ambitious and realistic, as my colleague loones also said. it has been 10 years since this regulation has been updated, so we feel very strongly that what we do now has to be future—proof and solid and should be able to last for quite a while, so what we do now we must do well and make it a robust mechanism that deals with all the loose ends. i also want to end by saying thank you to civil society and human rights organisations that have been very involved in helping us to push for these much—needed reforms. i think this highlights the importance of civil society also for us here in the european parliament.

outcome is a matter of political choice, political preference. there are no objective criteria, but how we manage the process is something that we all share in.
we are talking today about the budget, about the money that brussels is taking from the citizens. while the tax cuts are pouring in, more money is needed. all those billions have not bought a solution to the financial crisis, nor have they found a solution to the migration crisis. but critics of this are threatened with the withdrawal of subsidies. that is undemocratic and irresponsible and shows the immaturity of this parliament. where'd all that money go? some of them are to an airport in spain where no one has ever landed, or to a sound screen in poland where no one lives. and the money isn't being spent according to the rules. the court of auditors shall determine this again each year. in short, budgets are being spent inefficiently and incorrectly and money will not solve the asylum crisis either. my party will therefore vote against these proposals and call for a massive budget cut. let the eu stop this useless and costly waste.

i have a slightly different story than the last two speakers because the reports by rapporteurs ruas, blanco lópez and jakovčić show that the agreed eu 2020 objectives and the european structural and investment funds go hand in hand. with the €360 billion, we are investing in the heart of the eu 2020 targets and within the commitments of the stability and growth pact. as mrs crețu has also said, these are knowledge, innovation, the digital economy, small and medium-sized enterprises, the low-carbon and blue economy, energy and social inclusion, including for refugees. that is basically the end of this debate on 2016 and the adjustments to the multiannual financial framework and the eu 2020 agreements. more figures are on the table in early 2016. but at the same time, we also see that there are problems with implementation. application procedures have become too cumbersome. the control density is also increasing. the checks are piling up. that is why i ask commissioner crețu: will you also make proposals for simplification in the near future, where appropriate? the high level working group, led by vice-president and commissioner siim kallas, can help in this respect. the ep has already expressed its support for interim technical adjustments to the regulations. we have already prepared a resolution, with thanks to mr andrey novakov, which, after further consideration, can be voted on later this year.
today we are witnessing yet another attempt by brussels to undermine the sovereignty of the nation states. the elections to the european parliament must be made more european and less national, ostensibly because you are concerned about the democratic deficit in brussels, about the ever-decreasing turnout, about the fact that nobody knows who is the president of the european commission. the democratic deficit cannot be solved by fighting democracy in the member states with a ban on exit polls, with the obligation to put men and women on the electoral rolls again and again, with electoral thresholds. you'd better worry about why nobody cares, why only 42 percent of eligible voters feel called to go and vote. perhaps it has something to do with the alien attitude of the majority of the so-called representatives of the people who are seated here in this parliament. elections to the european parliament are elections of national representatives who, in my case, should represent the dutch people. these elections are held in the netherlands and are governed by the dutch electoral law. please do not interfere with that.
in the eu we have a single market with free movement of goods. gmo products are also covered. the new commission proposal would restrict the free movement of gmo products. this could mean, for example, reintroducing border controls and bypassing some member states by trucks. all too often we see files being blocked for legal or procedural reasons, which hampers businesses and end users. i am thinking here of the new plant breeding techniques, for example cisgenesis. unlike genetically modified organisms, only genes of the species are used. this is basically the same as conventional breeding, only many times faster. beneficial crop characteristics such as disease and drought resistance can thus be crossed more quickly and efficiently. this can reduce the use of plant protection products considerably. in 2012, the european food safety authority (efsa) concluded that cisgenesis is as safe as conventional breeding and that when only genes of the species are used in breeding, there is no gmo. however, cisgenese is still subject to strict gmo legislation, which still prevents the use of this promising technique. back to the proposal on the table. the consequences of the new proposal for the import of gmos are very harmful. it would be good if both the commission and the member states took their responsibility and made the present system work. i am convinced that the european parliament will take its responsibility by rejecting the proposal.
europe is making a mockery of food policy. the main aim of this novel food proposal is to enable the food industry to bring futuristic food to the european market as quickly and easily as possible. clone meat, nanomedicine, bug burgers, all of which are big money items. on the side, there are still efforts to protect animal welfare, food safety and the environment, but these measures are contradictory, fragmented and often overtaken by reality. take cloned meat, for example. the majority of european citizens and the european parliament are against animal cloning. but while a ban is still being worked on, this novel food proposal can be started with the pleasure of its approval. without a clear vision and without clear priorities, you cannot have a consistent policy. the welfare of animals, the environment and food safety, which are important european values, cannot be guaranteed. we cannot let the euro be the only european shared value, can we? i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
today we are discussing the regulation on novel foods. in this context, it is important to keep the door open to new technological developments. we're going to need it badly if we're going to continue to feed the world's population, especially in the future. the european market has become increasingly unified in recent years and i am therefore pleased that the new procedure facilitates the admission of new products to the market. this will make it easier to market new foods such as nanotechnology products and insects. how are we going to feed our population in a sustainable way if we don't think about new ways and how we can achieve that? the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we must give the developments i know from the netherlands, including the universities of twente and brabant, a chance and let them prove themselves. just being afraid is not going to get us anywhere. when people let fear drive them, they make the wrong decisions. the debate must be based on facts and not on emotions, and for that we need common sense. i would like to invite the critics to come to the netherlands to see what the state of play is in the field of nanotechnology.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. european air quality is affected by methane emissions in third countries. this is why the european commission has chosen to include methane in the nec directive to show the rest of the world that the eu has a clear methane target. i do not think that this is the right instrument. i think the following arguments are sufficient to exclude methane from the proposal. number one: methane is primarily a greenhouse gas and is therefore already regulated through climate targets. two , you 're not . the commission has already reduced methane emissions in europe by almost a third. three , please . we can reduce methane emissions from ruminants like cows and sheep through feed, but the only way to actually capture methane is to put animals back in the barn, and that's when society is asking us to keep the animals out. fourth . in particular, organically farmed animals emit more methane because they are fed a different diet. five , please . the commission has already taken a number of measures to reduce the greenhouse gas emissions of the european union. the commission has already proposed a number of amendments to the proposal. so we achieve exactly what we want when we give the agricultural entrepreneurs the space and flexibility to achieve the realistic objective.
on behalf of the verts/ale group.– madam president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, julie girling, for the very good cooperation we enjoyed. we might disagree on some issues in relation to air quality policies, but ms girling was a fair negotiator and a fair rapporteur, and we were able to reach a good position in the committee on environment, public health and food safety. that is the position we will defend here in the plenary vote. what the environment committee has produced is balanced, ambitious and pragmatic, and that is very important. we are here to fight to keep up that stance in order to open negotiations with the member states. i would like to mention two issues. the first is the higher ambition level. we have a european parliament impact assessment available showing that, with the same cost effectiveness, we can reduce our emissions more, and deliver more on public health. that finding has been reached with an impact assessment. people are always complaining that we just come up with numbers and put them where we like: well, this has been done on the basis of research. i think that is a very good thing, and we support the higher ambition level based on research. on the second point, agriculture, it is fascinating to hear that everyone is talking about air quality and the fact that we need source policies and that all sectors need to contribute, so for the first time we are addressing agriculture – and immediately there is a major lobby out there to get rid of any action in relation to agriculture. i have a major problem with that: every sector needs to contribute, and certainly also agriculture. we are looking forward to the negotiations after this vote.
i'm a calm and optimistic person. but when the european commission starts to interfere with figures, and in this case with the monitoring and standardization of emissions, then the damp sweat of fear breaks out in me. the commission has been tampering with figures for 20 years. how seriously can you take an institution which has not received a single approval of its expenditure from its own court of auditors in the last 20 years? it's the same as asking robert mugabe to check the elephant population in africa. totally out of luck. and this proposal is hopeless, too.
clean air is literally vital. air pollution is a very dangerous killer and we know that in europe thousands of people die prematurely every year from particulate matter. it is therefore important that we establish stricter standards, especially for particulate matter. at the same time, we must not fall into the trap of unnecessary rule-making. substances such as methane are already regulated in the climate package and i do not think that double regulation is desirable. let's stick to the rules. that's really crucial. at the same time, we have to puzzle over where the boundaries should be and ask ourselves if they are really achievable. it must not be that dutch cows will soon be unable to graze in the pasture because europe has introduced an ill-conceived rule. cows should be able to stand in the pasture and eat fresh grass instead of being inside in the stable and chewing imported soybeans. clean air is important, but not unnecessary rule-making.
this proposal is a direct hit to our productive sectors, such as industry, transport and agriculture. the inclusion of methane in the proposal constitutes a serious constraint on the reduction of greenhouse gases. there is over-regulation and this threatens to destroy many family farms in europe. since 1990, ammonia emissions have fallen by 30% in europe and by 66% in the netherlands. this is mainly due to the heavy efforts of family farms, which have suffered greatly under the environmental measures. it is not possible that the 2005 zero line will be used indiscriminately. the future air quality must be based on the efforts of the member states. that is necessary for a level playing field in europe. clean air may cost something, but don't let the same sectors pay for it all the time. one of the solutions must be manure processing and the use of phosphate as a valuable future scarce resource. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. i therefore ask the commissioner to take action on this. i have been doing that for 20 years as a director in politics and agriculture. this is how farmers know where they stand and can invest. and that's a very important point about clean air in this debate.
in writing. ‒ in the vote on the enlargement of the european court of justice, we chose to abstain on all amendments relating to the number of judges. it is important that the court of first instance should function properly, which is why enlargement is necessary. the original draft law therefore envisaged an extension of the existing 28 judges by 12 additional judges (1 per member state). unfortunately, the negotiations between the member states have shown that the national interest was considered to be so important that it was decided to extend the number of judges by 28 additional judges, i.e. a total of two per member state. other amendments to the proposal such as attention to gender equality, efficient organisation and a review clause are supported. we therefore did not want to jeopardise the compromise text, since our s&d group had to negotiate hard to get these points in. but because of the large number of judges, the additional costs involved and the question of whether this doubling is proportionate, we have therefore chosen to abstain on those specific points.

in writing. ‒ the vvd delegation believes that the novel food regulation is an improvement for food producers by simplifying, speeding up and centralising the procedure for placing novel foods on the market. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal. the vvd delegation expressed, however, its concern about the possible costs of such an authorization procedure for the producers of insects intended for human consumption already on the market in the netherlands. the european commission should therefore ensure a smooth and rapid transition with a minimum of administrative burden for the operators concerned. the fvo continues to monitor this situation very closely and is in contact with the producers concerned. legal uncertainty has led to this undesirable situation. the vvd delegation, however, believes that this new legislation creates legal clarity and benefits producers, and therefore votes in favour.
in writing. ‒ the vvd delegation believes that emission ceilings for methane have no place in the directive on national emissions of air pollutants. methane is a greenhouse gas and is already regulated in climate objectives agreements, including the effort sharing decision. it gives member states the flexibility to reduce greenhouse gas emissions on the basis of co2 equivalents. the inclusion of methane in the directive on national emission ceilings limits this flexibility, and the vvd delegation is against this. the vvd delegation welcomed the exclusion of methane emissions from ruminants but would have preferred that methane was not included in the european parliament's position at all.

last may, research by ghent university revealed that almost half of the planes operated by low-cost airlines such as ryanair are operated by mostly young freelancers on pay-to-fly contracts. cabin crew from south east asia, such as norwegian air, are also becoming more common. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the air transport services. these practices are bad for workers, pose risks to passenger safety and harm airlines that treat their employees with dignity. commissioner thijssen said earlier this week that precarious work in europe must be tackled. that is a big task which requires more work than any one commissioner can handle. that is why i ask commissioner bulc: what are you going to do to address the social abuses in international transport? for example, in the aviation package, will you link landing rights to just good work?
