on behalf of the alde group.– madam president, madam commissioner, the alde group welcomes the fact that this trade agreement and trade negotiations, which are the first of their kind between the eu and a developing country, vietnam, have been concluded. we think it is a good agreement. trade, as an element of the broader eu foreign policy agenda, is one of the more concrete instruments and one where parliament plays a leading and important role. but we should also think of development policies. we see, too, that citizens are increasingly and actively getting involved with this discussion, and we welcome this. people in europe are very clear that they want to see human rights at the top of the european foreign policy agenda. i believe that human rights are key. i think that the eu has both the opportunity and the responsibility to lead in ensuring that universal human rights are respected worldwide. while trade must be an important lever, it cannot in and of itself be the solution to end all suffering and problems. listening to the public debates, people sometimes have very high hopes of the impact that trade can have. but realistically, we must do what we can. i see this ambition and vision – tying trade and values – clearly reflected in the commission’s new trade for all agenda. besides the anticipated boost to development and economic well-being, building on vietnam’s efforts to lift people out of poverty, i would like to know what short-term measures are anticipated. how does the commission address human rights in the context of trade and how does monitoring look after the agreement enters into force? there is a lot that has to be improved in vietnam. free expression, free association, freedom of assembly are all controlled and under pressure; torture and beatings unfortunately happen too regularly; and minorities, journalists, bloggers and opposition figures suffer as a result of this. in conclusion, these many challenges cannot be solved through trade alone. we need to work and build on the human rights dialogue and show political leadership. i look forward to continuing this discussion and monitoring the impact this trade agreement will have on human rights in vietnam. i will continue to address it in other ways as well.
the suffering we cause animals is enormous and with a new animal welfare strategy europe can do something about it. the basis for this is already laid down in article 13 of the lisbon treaty, which says that animals are sentient beings, which we must take into account. but what are these words worth if the commission does not take any initiative in the field of animal welfare and leaves animals to their fate and to the operation of the market? what are those words worth when europe is a sanctuary for savage bullfights or even the burning of bulls? what are these words worth when the unstunned slaughter of animals is still allowed, when there are alternatives? and what are those words worth when the endless dragging of animals in overcrowded cattle cars in and out of europe is still allowed? what are those words worth if we do not prohibit the painful force-feeding of animals for the production of foie gras? the commission can tackle these abuses, mr president, not with a eurobarometer survey but simply with a new animal welfare strategy. but last week it already informed several ngos that it had no intention of coming up with a new strategy, and that was before this debate with parliament. that cannot be true, mr president! i hope that the commission will today show respect for the broad call from society and parliament to stand up for animals. they have a right to it. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
we are talking about the continuation of the animal welfare strategy. i am against it for the following reason. the directives and regulations must be implemented and we have those instruments in this parliament. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. many farmers in the netherlands have already implemented these directives and regulations. now we have an uneven playing field, high costs for the farmer and the citizen does not pay. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the common agricultural policy. we're talking about animal welfare here. but it should be about human welfare. we in europe let refugees walk for hours in the cold - and i am thinking of the elderly and small children - while animals are heated, ventilated and given water during transport. and we are talking about reducing driving time from eight hours to seven. in the netherlands, the cabinet says: if there's no room for refugees, people have to sleep outside in the cold, in the snow. the animals have been subjected to and have met stringent requirements as regards heating, cage area and lighting. is there nothing else for us in this house to do? the epp resolution on paris was not supported. let's get on with today's agenda item. let us help people and find solutions to the real crisis in europe, which is asylum policy, good reception and a joint approach to the terrorist movement isis. that is why human welfare should now be put on the agenda ahead of animal welfare, which i have always been a staunch supporter of. blue-card answer.– it is always fascinating to hear ukip talk about these kinds of things as soon as ‘green’ is in it. why exactly are you not making any point on the huge subsidy plans that are going to nuclear in hinkley point? billions of euros you want to use to subsidise hinkley nuclear. is it not about time to start discussing that issue instead of the renewables where the prices go down, where people have own ownership of energy? that is something that you should strive for, instead of just going for centralised nuclear and big subsidies there. it is you who are barking up the wrong tree.
mr president, from the far left to the far right, we are all concerned about jobs and growth in the european union, and rightly so. let’s face it: the competitiveness of our industry is also one of the central themes in the whole debate on the energy union, and it is obvious that europe needs cheap, clean and independent energy. desperately clinging to our fossil past will definitely not create competitiveness in the future. we see that new disruptive technologies are overhauling europe’s energy system. prices of solar technology are dropping sharply and energy efficiency is decoupling economic growth from our energy use. key to affordable energy is giving certainties to investors. energy infrastructure requires large, risky capital-intensive investments, and only governance arrangements based on eu legal mechanisms can create this stability and predictability. the current energy union is still far away from legislative action based on democratic decision-making. the energy union is, unfortunately, based on voluntary submissions from member states, and the commission should be more ambitious here. but the main obstacle is again the member states. they seem to prefer having full ownership of their own national energy policies with expensive pollutive energy systems to being part of a full-scale european approach with astronomical benefits, and the absence of the council here today is exemplary. let us care about jobs and growth in europe and push our member states towards a true european energy union.
in writing. ‒ although the name of the commission for the conservation of southern bluefin tuna (ccsbt) suggests otherwise, the ccsbt is mainly concerned with the exploitation of tuna and not with the protection of tuna. the ccsbt sets annual quotas for bluefin tuna in the southern hemisphere oceans, whereas bluefin tuna is threatened with extinction and fishing should be stopped. the ccsbt policy is nothing more than destructive mispolicy: the ccbst offers no prospect of recovery for bluefin tuna. europe is likely to defend the interests of french and spanish tuna fishermen, but it is also expected to counterbalance the ccsbt policy, which is now mainly determined by japan and korea. i voted for this report in the hope that the eu, as a member of the ccbts, will be able to stop the increase in quotas and will pursue a total ban on the catching of bluefin tuna. i will be closely monitoring the future eu contribution to the ccbts.
in writing. ‒ child poverty is a serious problem. it's a shame that children still live in poverty. the party for the animals stands up for the very weakest in our society. but the fight against child poverty by brussels must not be misused to impose european social and economic policies on member states. this is at the expense of the sovereignty of the member states. we in the netherlands are quite capable of defining our own social and economic policies and have good social security standards. we do not need a european reform agenda to undermine this. however, i wholeheartedly support the call from europe to tackle child poverty.
many multinational corporations, such as starbucks, facebook and philip morris, are deliberately avoiding taxes. the special committee on tax rulings has again brought this to light. my own country, the netherlands, made this possible by secret agreements that were on the fringes of the law. i call that unethical. after all, all these companies benefit from social facilities such as infrastructure, vigilant police forces and trade agreements, but they do not pay for them. companies, consumers and governments must ensure that corporate social responsibility is also a reality for these multinationals. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tax system is fair and that the tax on profits is paid where the profits are made. taxation is and remains a core competence of the member states and competition between member states in the field of taxation helps governments to remain sharp and lean. i do not want european tariffs, but we can make a big difference here in europe in terms of transparency and definitions.
perhaps in addition to what the previous speaker said. i am not known in this house as someone who is sick of a little competition, but if that competition means that we are all involved in a race to absolute zero, i think that is not wise. so i welcome the work that this committee has done. i think that this committee - and i would like to thank the rapporteurs and the chairman - has worked largely on the same basic principle, and that is fairness. how fair is it when citizens pay taxes, when smes pay their fair share and then big companies jump the gun across the border? yes, the groups differ in their views on what is needed next. what is proportional? what should you do, what shouldn't you do? but we in this parliament are for the most part agreed that the solution, despite the fact that taxation is a national matter, must also be found in europe. and the report makes some very useful suggestions to that end, which i wholeheartedly support. the european commission is thus given a very important task, namely to put together, to fit together 28 national pieces of the puzzle. the responsibility, dear commission, to dare to take action when necessary. that has been lacking in the past. and that is what i miss in the debate, and i may have to agree with the previous speaker on the subject of competition. of course, we do not operate in a vacuum. the european union is in a global context, and i miss the story: are we looking at the competitive position enough? not of one individual country, but of the european union as a whole. this is a good start. and get back to work.
thank you to the rapporteurs and the chairman of the special committee on tax rulings. there is an excellent report that shows that a huge financial, legal complex has been created that allows big companies, the very richest, to siphon off their profits and to withdraw their assets from the tax authorities. that makes fairness a long shot. the disparities are very, very large. the coffee house can't compete fairly with starbucks. the common man or woman does not have the opportunities that the very richest have, and that affects tax morality. developing countries are looking into it. they see the much-needed tax dollars disappearing across the border. those are the victims. there are also perpetrators, and those perpetrators should be better named in the report. the culprits are the big corporations that claim to be socially responsible but have no trouble creating tax structures. the perpetrators are also the tax advisors who advise the big corporations or the tax havens that help to steal the profits or make the assets disappear. and the perpetrators are also the member states, here in europe, which are a gateway to these tax havens, including member states such as luxembourg and the netherlands. these countries must take the lead. i agree with esther de lange that the commission has an important role to play now. it must take initiatives and encourage the member states to put an end to these practices. let this report be the beginning of the end.
in paris, islam has once again shown its true face, the face of evil, in a monstrous manner. that makes me sad, but mostly it makes me angry. angry, because i wonder how many more victims will have to fall before the eyes of the onlookers are opened. angry, because i wonder when you'll stop making excuses for islamic terrorism. the problem is not the economic crisis, unemployment or not being at home at school. the west is under attack, and you're proposing to fight terrorism with awareness campaigns, school lectures, and tea with imams. you can't solve radicalization by hugging it. you can't solve that with millions of dollars of deradicalization projects. you don't solve that by sticking your head in the sand about the dangers of islam. the only cure for radicalization is de-islamicizing, pre-emptively detaining potential terrorists. jihadists who return are not treated as traumatized youths but as persona non grata. and if you want to go to syria, go, but don't come back. and finally, national states must close their borders and defend the freedom and security of their citizens.
in 2004, filmmaker theo van gogh was murdered in amsterdam. i was a member of the amsterdam city council at the time. he was killed by a radical muslim, an extremist. i was there. fortunately, i was not there at the time, but i was there when we had to worry in amsterdam about how to deal with extremists. turns out this extremist was being bugged at home by the secret service and they knew he was radicalizing. it was a boy from amsterdam with a dutch passport. and in the years that followed, there were the attacks in london, madrid, brussels, paris and toulouse, each time people with european passports on the intelligence services' radar. what we need to realise is that countering terrorism in europe means we need to understand why our boys are becoming radicalised and that means we need to be in the neighbourhoods and not cut back on policing and investigation.
good health and a decent working environment are fundamental social rights in europe. despite all the fine words and agreements, too many people still get sick at work. an ambitious policy on health and safety at work contributes to the productivity of companies and thus also to the sustainability of our social security systems, whereas the costs of not having such a policy are very high. in short, there is no excuse for not protecting workers. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment and the environment from the adverse effects of certain substances is a good one. i would ask the commission to ensure that the study of precarious work includes the effects of precarious work on working conditions.
4,000 workers die each year as a result of an accident at the workplace. three million people are unable to work for three days or more because of an accident at work. 25% of europeans feel that their safety or health at work is at risk. all in all, there are sufficient reasons for this debate to take place here tonight and i thank commissioner thyssen very much for her clear statement on this point. i think that the three priorities she has indicated are very important. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to support them. marianne thyssen spoke about the labour inspectorates which must play a fundamental role in this and i sometimes wonder whether all labour inspectorates in europe are adequately equipped to fulfil that role. i would like to hear her thoughts on that and also how we might improve it. and then, as a final point, marianne thyssen also spoke about this and it's something that's close to my heart: the position of people with chronic pain, the position of people with neurological disorders, and the position of people with muscular disorders. people who often have a double burden, not just because of the disease, but also because they are dealing with stigma and problems in the workplace, whereas those very problems in the workplace can often be prevented with simple adjustments. i would also ask that you continue to pay close attention to this.

madam president, after every attack, like everybody else, i feel grief, shock, horror, and anger at the murderers. i also feel mounting frustration because what follows is a familiar ritual by now. first, we get a lot of solemn statements about our shared values (but quite frankly, i have heard some statements here that make me doubt whether we have shared values). then we hear a lot of solemn statements about democratic values, fundamental rights and the rule of law. much of this is lip service, because a raft of new measures is announced after every single attack, but their effectiveness is doubtful. the council and commission have so far refused an evaluation on the basis of article 7, as requested by this house. maybe now they will finally do it. implementation of the measures by the member states is patchy and inadequate. then there is a near obsession with electronic mass surveillance – whereas what is needed is much more investment in human intelligence, in people on the ground who know what is going on in the banlieues and in molenbeek. why do we tolerate the existence of ghettos in our cities that are no-go areas for the police – where the police do not even dare to get out of their cars – so they cannot know what is going on? let us start investing in human intelligence. lastly, with regard to the passenger name record (pnr) proposal, i am asking the council and the commission to stop the campaign of lies. it is a lie to suggest that this house, or even – as is sometimes claimed – the centre-left groups in it, are blocking a pnr system. we have voted, we are committed, we are working on this issue: but commitment does not mean a blank cheque, ok? this is democracy: we are entitled to our views. we are negotiating, and now it is for the council to move. the ball is in their court.
the horrific images of the cowardly attack in paris will haunt our minds for a long time. so many victims, so many young people whose lives were abruptly ended in a concert hall, a restaurant or on the street. it's only natural that they're looking at politics. it is only natural that europe should be considered. we all have a great responsibility here, parliament from left to right, the member states, the commission, to guarantee the safety of our citizens and it is high time we took up that responsibility. it is not a question of new proposals, as some of my colleagues would have us believe. these are proposals that were on our agenda long before paris: pnr, the counter-terrorism centre, the internet reporting unit, the mandate of europol, things we have sometimes been talking about for years. although some colleagues have tried to do so again here today, no european citizen can be explained that.

in writing. ‒ the eu budget for 2016 represents an increase of 1.8% compared to the 2015 budget. that's well above the inflation rate of 0.11%. the large number of people coming to the eu for asylum and migration requires more resources from the member states and the eu to address these issues. with this in mind, the eu is rightly cutting back in several other policy areas, but it should have done so much more. this also applies to the european parliament's budget, which is 2.4% higher than this year. the substantial increase in the eu budget shows that the eu still wants to do too much, including delivering economic growth and jobs, by doing the best that member states can. the crises of recent years should have made europe realize that it could do better by concentrating on its core tasks: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the european union's action to include crossborder issues where the eu needs to deliver, such as working with member states to deal compassionately and fairly with refugees and migrants, and effectively tackling the persistent problems of the euro and excessive debt levels. it is absolutely positive that commissioner georgieva is really going to set priorities, but that should lead to a more efficient budget.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of this report because it contains a number of positive ideas. we know from history that extremism does not belong to any particular religion, ideology or population group, as some in this house would have us believe. this report recognises the importance of participation and engagement in the community, based on trust, so that signals can be recognised and reported in a timely manner without an entire community being seen as suspect. national security depends on security in the neighborhood, and local organizations need adequate resources, as does the ran network. we need to improve the use of our existing intelligence and strengthen agencies such as europol. that is a much better investment than collecting flight passenger name records (pnr), which i do not support. i also express my concern about some of the proposals for tackling extremism on the internet. i want illegal material removed as soon as possible, but within a legal framework. encryption is a tool that can be both positive and negative. eu citizens recruited for violent extremism pose a danger both within and beyond our borders. cooperation with third countries should be based on clear principles.

the attacks in paris, the war in syria, the russian fighter plane shot down by a nato member, the total chaos in libya and, oh yes, the refugee crisis. we have a lot to worry about and you would say that right now european member states should count their blessings and invest in cooperation with each other and with neighbouring countries. this is no time to be selfish or opinionated. and when i hear the high commissioner say that this was a summit not about simple bribery but about partnership, i think that was the summit you wanted to have and that i wanted to have too. but when i look back, i see nothing different from the european member states which were selfish, which were one-sided, a summit which ended in: how can we use development money already earmarked to stop immigrants? a summit where member states were not willing to pay any money. a summit where we basically embezzled money. we've been playing with the pots that were already there. european countries were hoping to make deals with african countries to take back migrants, to set up asylum processing centers beyond our borders, to strengthen border controls. and in the search for quick solutions, member states seem willing to turn a blind eye to human rights situations in countries with dictatorial regimes such as eritrea or sudan or countries with military regimes that are almost indistinguishable from dictatorships, such as egypt. fleeing violence and oppression and bribing the oppressor may hold a refugee back for a short time, but in the long run the situation becomes unmanageable. isn't that what we learned from the past? the expectations of the naive euro-friendly elite for the valetta summit were high, because deals would be made with african countries to take back illegal immigrants immediately. the flow of illegal immigrants from africa would now be really curbed. but, mr president, of course, in line with the general failure of the european union, none of that has come to pass. because, of course, all of africa was after one thing. more money from the europeans and they got more money. hundreds of millions of taxpayers' money on top of the billions that africa is already getting was pumped into a trust fund. but it was not just tax money that was poured in to keep african countries happy. no, africa also gets easier visa procedures. this summit has turned out to be a big fiasco. billions are going to africa again, and as a thank you, even more african fortune seekers are coming to the european union. and if it's up to my party, the freedom party, no more money goes to africa and we spend our tax money in our own country and we close our borders.
expectations were high: the european council in brussels on 14 and 15 december adopted a resolution on the situation in the middle east. it should have been an opportunity to formulate a common migration agenda with more than 60 countries. unfortunately, this opportunity was not seized and the summit ended in disappointment. the eu showed no willingness to tackle the root causes of migration, such as poverty, conflict, lack of employment, in a proactive manner. and african leaders were therefore unwilling to make deals on, for example, the mandatory return of rejected asylum seekers to europe, something that eu countries were hammering on. as we have unfortunately seen time and again in recent months, the european leaders have again failed to show the political courage, ambition and determination that will be needed if we are to tackle the refugee problem in a decent and effective way. there will be a rematch this weekend at the eu-turkey summit. turkey plays a key role in bringing the influx of asylum seekers into the eu under control. it is high time that european governments showed that they are willing to contribute to solutions and not just sign treaties and then do nothing about them.
on behalf of the s&d group.– mr president, the violence in burundi keeps escalating. human rights defenders, journalists and their family members are attacked and murdered. since president nkurunziza insisted on continuing with his third term as president, and the politically-motivated killings started in april, at least 240 citizens have been killed. 215 000 burundians have fled the country and many people are internally displaced. public executions and silent killings continue. we saw that also during the last three weeks. i welcome the un security council resolution finally adopted on 12 november calling upon the government of burundi and all parties to reject any kind of violence and to immediately convene an inclusive and genuine dialogue with the ruling party and the opposition, both inside and outside of the country. but the sad thing today is that we could see this coming for years, and so it could have been prevented, but the international community chose to look away for way too long, even deciding to close the united nations office in burundi (bnub) six months before crucial elections. now it is our duty to act so that a full-blown civil war in the country is prevented. the latest elections that were held in burundi are not recognised by the international community, and the violence continues. so what is the eu doing in order to put pressure on the current government to comply with the un security resolution?
the commission has also proposed that the council should be able to take a decision on the matter. indeed, all sorts of people were not allowed to vote. i also share mr tannock's analysis of whether mrs aung san suu kyi will make room for minorities. but despite all the criticism, what we saw was an election day that was incredibly well organized. i must especially commend the young ladies who staff all those polling stations. it's like they're holding elections every day. we saw after the elections that the military accepted the gains of the nld. that is good news for the beginning of a new parliamentary democracy. but we're a long way from there. the military has had 25 years to shape the constitution in such a way that it will be a battle before we can talk about real democracy here, and that is a process in which we as the european parliament could also play a part. we have decided together that myanmar is one of the countries where we want to help parliamentary democracy. it is indeed my task to shape this together with you and that will be quite a job. but it is a parliament where parliamentarians are not seated by political group but in alphabetical order, and if that is the case then parliamentary democracy with a multiparty system is still a long way off.
mr president, georgia is, in many respects, a success story. it is the only country in the region that managed a peaceful transition from one government to another – a transition effected in the electoral booths rather than on the streets. furthermore, georgia is one of the three countries in the eastern neighbourhood which have stated that they want a closer relationship with the eu. the association agreement is the fundament of this – a relationship based on values. the rule of law, human rights and media freedom are at the core of these values. therefore it is crucial that even the perception of political interference with the judiciary and a free press is avoided at all times. in this regard, to put it diplomatically, the case of rustavi 2 should have been handled differently. but it is likewise worrying how former president saakashvili tried to instrumentalise this case to instigate unrest, which is the last thing georgians need right now. georgia has to deliver on reforms. furthermore, there so much instability around the country that it cannot afford deep internal divisions. the threats are external. three years after the democratic change of government, it is high time for the political forces in the country to unite. an important tangible result of eu-georgia cooperation might be the abolition of visa requirements for all georgian citizens, but that could come under threat if the unhealthy political rivalry in the country continues. are there still problems in georgia? yes, certainly. to name a few: the winner-takes-all mentality continues to prevail; the most influential political actor in georgia is not accountable to anyone; and radical groups keep popping up, although it is a mystery who is financing them. nevertheless i am confident that, with a genuine effort by all the political forces in the country, the necessary reforms already started under the previous administration can continue, and georgian citizens can soon reap the benefits of closer cooperation with the eu.

many people in europe are unable or barely able to keep their heads above water and have been hit by the storm of the economic crisis. it is therefore the task of european politicians to get the economy on its feet so that it can weather new storms. the european commission is laying sandbags. the attention paid to the budgetary impact for the euro area as a whole is good. the proposal to complete the banking union is also welcome, as is the recommendation to countries with current account surpluses to encourage investment. yet, the sandbags show exactly where the holes in the dikes are. budgetary prudence and financial fragmentation have made adjustment slow, unbalanced and therefore costly in terms of growth and high unemployment. it seems that the ecb is the only one responsible for getting the economy back on track. moreover, the potential for growth and jobs is lower than ever. investment is 20% lower than before and public investment has been cut, while interest rates are lower than ever. showing sandbags is not enough. we need a european delta plan for a democratic and united eurozone so that everyone can keep their heads above water and a new storm cannot threaten us again.
in writing. ‒ the decision to abstain as a member of the pvda in the vote on paragraph 32 of this resolution is based on two considerations. firstly, this paragraph is merely positive about the opportunities that an energy chapter in ttip could bring to the eu, without naming the concerns, reservations or conditions to be attached to such a chapter. the energy chapter of the trade agreement with the usa should also deal with products which pollute the environment, such as shale gas and tar sands oil. secondly, the fact that the americans are shielding their fossil fuels from the world market is one of the eu's offensive interests in the negotiations. if ttip can provide europe with access to us oil reserves, it will make europe less dependent on the middle east and russia for its oil and gas. this paragraph did not meet the concerns of the european parliament's pvda delegation, but we do not want to reject the inclusion of an energy chapter in ttip, which is why i abstained.

on behalf of the alde group.– madam president, on behalf of my whole group – mr duncan, please note – i would like to call this long-awaited agreement a truly historic agreement. it is legally binding, in so far as international agreements can be. it has a strong long-term ambition of 2°c, but it actually mentions 1.5°c, not forgetting the five—year review mechanism and the strengthening action that comes with it. it is an irreversible step towards a low—carbon economy and – even more importantly – it is a huge signal to the financial world that only zero-carbon solutions will pay off. that is truly important. let me also congratulate both minister dieschbourg, through mr gira, and commissioner arias cañete for their important role in paris. the eu truly built a bridge between the developing world and the developed world, and that showed in the end. however, to draw attention to one negative point as well, i believe that europe unnecessarily endangered the outcome by again being too reluctant on committing to financial donations. again it showed how important it is that we have our own financial mechanisms through which donations from europe do not depend on the mood of our ministers of finance. it is true that paris is just paper. the real work starts today. the most important thing is that all 195 countries that are going to sign the agreement implement their national plans, and we have to monitor that implementation. this applies to the eu as well. therefore my final question is what does paris mean for the eu’s own policies in the future? i urge the commission very rapidly to analyse the paris outcome and to see what it means for our 2020 objectives, our 2030 objectives, and even what it means for 2050 because what we do know is that the figure of 1.5°c is linked to the upper limits of our 2050 target of 95%, and that has to be translated into all other policies.
the climate agreement was adopted by consensus in paris last saturday. the french presidency deserves credit for this. never before have 195 countries pledged to work on climate change. and the approach of countries deciding what they can contribute, the indc, has certainly helped. the treaty calls for "two degrees and the aim of 1.5 degrees". that's a good start, but let's not fool ourselves into thinking we're already there. i remind you that we are already at a temperature rise of almost 1° celsius. the philippines is currently experiencing a hurricane. small islands are worried about their survival. president. - i welcome this agreement, let me be clear. but i also think the fossil fuel industry lobbyists are getting a big christmas bonus because of this climate deal. the agreement is not really binding and the reins are not in the balance. the fossil fuel industry is allowed to continue to make profits from oil. we're not going to stop producing carbon, we're going to capture it and store it. air and sea transport are not covered by this convention. it's business as usual with all the risks that go with it. and the absence of a compensation mechanism for developing countries is also a missed opportunity and an embarrassment to the western world. we are still evading our historical responsibility. this agreement should be a starting point for further action, not an end point. we need more ambition. we need to move away from fossil fuels and towards alternative, sustainable sources of energy. we need to be wise and more economical with our energy. insulating homes would be a good start, but it's also a lot of work. it is therefore regrettable that commissioner cañete has already said that he does not want to increase the targets for 2020. i call on the commissioner to take action now, when the momentum is there and everyone feels that something has been achieved. don't listen to your friends in the oil industry too much. european efforts are moving in the right direction. we're meeting our 2020 goals. let's commit to a 50% reduction in co2 emissions by 2030.
madam president, first of all, i would like to thank commissioner arias cañete for his job well done in paris. we know that we have been critical now and then, but if you do a job right we will also acknowledge that, so thank you very much, commissioner, from the green group too, for your job well done. the same goes for carole dieschbourg, and i am sure you will pass on to her the compliment for getting the job done. but, this now means, of course, that we have to start our work at european level. we all know that the agreement in paris states we want to stay well below 2°c, with efforts to achieve a ceiling of 1.5°c. that means something, because all eu policies at this moment are based on the intergovernmental panel on climate change (ipcc) scenario that gives a 50% chance of staying below 2°c. all the targets we set are for a 50% chance of staying below 2°c. so if we now agree to stay well below 2°c, and even to achieve 1.5°c, it really means we have to step up our own efforts. in that sense, commissioner arias cañete, i was in a good mood after the weekend but then i heard your press conference yesterday, and it was a bit disappointing to hear you say, more or less, that we would leave the extra ambition to the next commission. no! to say that would really be to burn your legacy one day after paris – and that would be a pity. what you need to say is, first of all, that there was a pledge that europe would move to minus 30% in 2020 if there was a global deal. well, there is a global deal: so europe can move to minus 30%. secondly, we need an impact assessment. what does 1.5°c mean – especially bearing in mind that, this morning, parliament adopted a binding 40% efficiency target for 2030. what does that mean? there too we need more information. on aviation also there was a slight mistake in your presentation yesterday. if the international civil aviation organisation (icao) does not deliver next year, then that means the eu will re-include aviation in the emissions trading scheme right away. no discussion – it is just the law – we will put it in the ets if icao does not deliver.
the participants in the paris conference are patting each other on the back for an agreement which has yet to prove its worth. because let's look back to kyoto. what did it cost us? the amount of the loan is eur 1 200 billion. and what did it accomplish? the warming was prevented by 0.017°. so not a great score. but there will be enough people who will benefit from this agreement. because the green entrepreneurs, as they like to call themselves, will see their profits increase with the environmental demands in their hands; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that it is not able to meet the needs of the people of europe. the taxpayers, and especially the dutch taxpayer. so mr eickhout, if he already pays taxes in the netherlands, you will pay. so on behalf of the dutch taxpayer, thank you very much!

- i'm the reporter. first of all, i would like to express my sincere thanks to the shadow rapporteurs and all others directly involved in this eu/china report for the constructive, valuable cooperation. the report deserves the support of parliament, as it addresses the crucial eu-china relations in their full breadth and depth. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. in writing this report, i gratefully drew on the expertise of foreign correspondents in china. their reporting contributes to the public image of china in europe to a considerable extent. however, the serious obstacles faced by foreign journalists in china are counterproductive. of course, chinese media can now operate in europe with complete freedom. we are fully behind that. hence my unsuccessful plea for an amendment for mutual media freedom between the eu and china. i regret this omission in the report. a previous report did mention it. equally important to the report was the academic input i received. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. inaccurate western translations or portrayals of chinese political positions can be misleading. the report mentions two cases of this: it is about the universality of human rights and the rule of law. as far as the rule of law is concerned, the chinese government does not object to this western translation, but president xi jinping does not leave any ambiguity about the fact that his country will not adopt a western style of law. and as for china's position on the scope of human rights, it was officially stressed recently this summer that beijing is committed to, and i quote, "combining the principle of the universality of human rights with the reality of china's national circumstances". both examples underline the need for europe to know and recognize chinese political positions accurately in order to ensure an adequate and fair development of relations. the eu and china, in their strategic partnership, can mean a great deal for each other's citizens, i have no doubt about that. the report goes into great detail on this. but this requires an open attitude on both sides, both to oneself and to the other. a solid foundation for this mutual openness is above all a sincere interest in each other and in the mutual welfare. i hope that this report will deepen and strengthen european-chinese relations!

hundreds of thousands of asylum seekers have found their way to the european union in the past year and the asylum tsunami is also sweeping my country. so far, more fortune seekers have entered the netherlands than in all of 2013 and 2014 combined, with all the consequences of that. this has got to stop. the netherlands is a functioning state of law where suspects, criminals, terrorists and asylum seekers are treated fairly. and yes, we reserve the right to place asylum seekers in immigration detention. alien detention is important. if it is up to my party, the party for freedom, the safety of the dutch citizen comes first and we apply this to both asylum seekers who are not screened for safety and to those who are processed or illegal. we shut them down or we lock them up. and we are the ones who decide that, not the european union. the pvv would prefer these asylum seekers not to come at all. the netherlands must close its borders.
the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment is a very good one. if not, then there is a real race to nature's attributes. it is a stalemate for the sector and i also think it is ethically wrong. plant material has no place in patent law; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used effectively. the basic principle of the breeder's right is the full breeder's exemption. this allows growers to create variety upon variety. we must continue to safeguard this basic principle. commissioner, "no patent on life" is the word. i and my colleagues received a petition with that title with over 17,000 signatures. we are happy to pass on this petition to your colleague, commissioner bieńkowska. commissioner, it is high time that the loophole in the legislation is closed. clarity is necessary and urgent. the resolution calls on the commission to do so swiftly and properly. the commission is now taking action.
growers use natural properties of fruit and vegetables and other plants to grow further. this is used to create new flavors or colors in, for example, tulips or tomatoes. recently it has become possible to patent these natural properties. this is dangerous, and both growers and consumers can suffer. when someone naturally breeds a new trait in a broccoli or a tomato, you don't want someone to be able to patent it. you create a monopoly on a property of a vegetable or fruit and therefore also on our food supply. the natural properties of plants are no longer freely available to all growers. this jeopardises innovation and may have consequences for the variety of varieties. our small and medium-sized growers, farmers and gardeners are among the world's leading breeders of new varieties and developers of new seeds. seeds that are sometimes worth more than gold by weight. if you've really invented something, you should be able to patent it. but the natural properties of plants must be freely exploited. i raised this matter with the committee on agriculture two months ago. i am glad that a large majority of the european parliament now supports this call. i also call on dutch state secretary van dam to take action during the dutch presidency of the european union. the european parliament is loud and clear. the commission must give a lot of room for innovation and ensure that the natural properties of plants remain available to everyone.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group vegetables, grains, legumes and fruit are the basis of a healthy and wholesome diet. these are of excellent quality in europe and for centuries farmers and later breeders have been storing the seeds and products every year to make new and improved products. for example, the breeding of those vegetable varieties has resulted in hundreds of varieties of potatoes. some are suitable for fries, some are suitable for mashed potatoes, and others are very suitable for growing in dry or very wet soil. we have a very, very proud sector and it is important that we keep these products, the seeds, freely available. that's important for biodiversity, that's important for innovation in this sector. and that cannot happen if agro-food companies apply for patents on products that are derived from essentially biological processes. nature's qualities belong to no one, or perhaps to everyone. but they certainly don't belong to monsanto and syngenta. it would be bad if these multinationals were to decide which varieties we should or should not grow in europe. these companies don't want competition and the market has no ethics. mr president, it is beyond words! farmers simply need to have free access to these seeds. that means, for example, an extensive grower exemption in european regulations. because if we continue down this path, we're going to create monopoly positions in our food supply, and those positions of power and the resulting loss of biodiversity are threats to our food security here and elsewhere in the world. commission, do your job, get a breeder exemption in the european patent rules. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
mr president, thanks to the development of new plant varieties, our farmers and gardeners are now able to feed 7 billion people. the preservation of small-scale breeders and their knowledge is of the utmost importance in order to guarantee employment and food security in the future. this knowledge is in danger of being lost now that the multinationals dominate the market. the cause is patent law. by patenting natural plant characteristics, the pond for small breeders is drying up. the commissioner, by granting only 20 patents, has already placed 900 varieties under the exclusive rights of the patent holders. that's a fact. it is almost always powerful non-european companies that have the capital to buy up plant traits on a massive scale, and then i think of monsanto. it was agreed in the breeders' agreement that no one should be prevented from developing varieties. that basic right has been violated. mr president, if the european commission does not act now, the processing industry will be monopolized. there is great unrest in the netherlands, i have brought this up many times here and i hope for a clear answer from our commissioner.
